Study the full derivation

Solving a two-point boundary problem without guessing the start slope · Dynamics and numerical time integration

When endpoint conditions act on different sides of an interval, shooting can amplify a tiny initial error. Construct a cubic collocation system, test its continuous differential residual and quantify which boundary-value solution the residual actually certifies.

Chapter 12 · Differential–algebraic equations and consistency

Derive a reduced evolution when an algebraic variable can be eliminated and find the hidden velocity constraint in a mechanical example.

From first principles

Determine which initial states can satisfy an algebraic constraint

A differential–algebraic model may evolve \(y\) while determining \(z\) instantaneously through a constraint. In a semi-explicit model, \(y'=f(t,y,z)\) specifies a rate and \(g(t,y,z)=0\) specifies admissible states. Initial data must satisfy the latter before the first time step. A differentiable solution must remain on it, hence the time derivative along the trajectory vanishes:

\[0=\frac{d}{dt}g(t,y,z)=g_t+g_y f+g_z\dot z .\]

If the square matrix \(g_z\) is invertible near the initial state, the constraint locally determines \(z\) from \(t,y\), and the hidden rate is

\[\dot z=-g_z^{-1}(g_t+g_y f).\]

For example impose the unit circle \(y^2+z^2=1\) with \(y'=u(t)\). On its upper half \(z=\sqrt{1-y^2}\), giving \(\dot z=-yu/z\). Algebraic consistency fixes \(z(0)\) once the branch and \(y(0)\) are known. At \(z=0\) this coordinate elimination becomes singular even though the circle itself is smooth. A constrained implicit time step imposes the original algebraic equation at the new time, which prevents the numerical drift that can accumulate from integrating only its differentiated version.

Derivation and applications

Derive the constraint force of a pendulum without eliminating coordinates

A solver using Cartesian coordinates for a pendulum must satisfy geometric, velocity and acceleration constraints. Let \(\mathbf r=(x,y)\) be the displacement from a fixed pivot, \(\mathbf v=\dot{\mathbf r}\), \(m\) the bob mass, and \(L\) the fixed link length. The applied force \(\mathbf F\) excludes the constraint reaction. Write the reaction as \(-\lambda\mathbf r\), so positive \(\lambda\) corresponds to an inward force and has units of N/m. The holonomic constraint and its first derivative are

\[g=\tfrac12(\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf r-L^2)=0,\]
\[\dot g=\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf v=0.\]

Differentiating once more shows that the acceleration is generally not tangent: maintaining curvature of the trajectory requires an inward normal component. Insert Newton's law and keep the known tangential speed:

\[\ddot g=|\mathbf v|^2+\mathbf r\cdot\dot{\mathbf v}=0,\]
\[m\dot{\mathbf v}=\mathbf F-\lambda\mathbf r .\]

Solving for the scalar multiplier gives both the required magnitude and the tangentially projected forcing:

\[\lambda=\frac{m|\mathbf v|^2+\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf F}{L^2},\]
\[\dot{\mathbf v}=\frac{\mathbf F}{m}-\frac{\lambda}{m}\mathbf r .\]

The multiplier can be found only after compatible position and velocity are supplied. Satisfying the position constraint alone at the initial time does not remove radial velocity. For a rigid rod either sign of \(\lambda\) is admissible. A string goes slack when the bilateral model would require compression.

Worked example

Calculate a reaction at the bottom of a swing

Choose \(y\) upward, \(m=0.200\) kg, \(L=0.500\) m, \(\mathbf r=(0,-0.500)\) m and \(\mathbf v=(1.00,0)\) m/s. Gravity is \((0,-1.962)\) N. Thus \(\lambda=[0.200+0.981]/0.250=4.724\) N/m, giving tension \(\lambda L=2.36\) N. The net upward acceleration is \((2.362-1.962)/0.200=2.00\) m/s², independently equal to \(v^2/L\).

Second application

Detect an inconsistent input and a physically slack string

At the same bottom position, a supplied velocity \((1.00,0.100)\) m/s has \(\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf v=-0.0500\) m²/s. Removing its radial projection gives \((1.00,0)\) m/s before evaluating the multiplier. At the top, instead take \(\mathbf r=(0,0.500)\) m and tangential speed \(1.00\) m/s. The bilateral equation predicts

\[\lambda L=0.400-1.962=-1.56\,\mathrm{N}.\]

A string cannot provide that outward push, so a taut-string simulation must release the constraint there.

A state has fewer independent coordinates than entries

A semi-explicit system has \(y'=f(t,y,z)\) and \(0=g(t,y,z)\). The algebraic equation supplies a condition at every instant; choosing every initial coordinate independently can violate it before time integration starts. If \(g_z\) is square and nonsingular locally, the implicit function theorem determines \(z=\psi(t,y)\) and substitution gives an ordinary differential equation for \(y\). Differentiating the constraint gives \(g_z z'=-g_t-g_y f\), so one differentiation determines the missing derivative. This is the usual semi-explicit index-one situation.

For \(y'=-y+z,\quad0=y+2z-t\), one has \(z=(t-y)/2\) and \(y'=(t-3y)/2\). If \(y(0)=2\), consistency forces \(z(0)=-1\) and \(y'(0)=-3\). Differentiating the constraint gives

\[z'(0)=(1-y'(0))/2=2.\]

Entering both initial coordinates as zero and two in the wrong order would describe a different trajectory even if a numerical solver silently repaired their algebraic inconsistency.

Discretise both conditions at the same time

A backward Euler step at \(h=0.2\) enforces \(y_1-2=0.2(-y_1+z_1)\) and \(y_1+2z_1=0.2\). Eliminating \(z_1\) gives \(1.3y_1=2.02\), so \(y_1\approx1.553846\) and \(z_1\approx-0.676923\). Computing the second variable from the new time matters. Using \(z_0\) throughout the update and reporting it as the new algebraic variable would violate the current constraint. Report its residual together with the local or global accuracy estimate for \(y\).

Hidden constraints and the force multiplier

For a unit-mass pendulum with Cartesian position \(q\), velocity \(v\), rod length \(L\) and downward gravity vector \(g\), take \(q'=v,\ v'=g-\lambda q\) and \(q^Tq=L^2\). Differentiating the rod constraint once imposes \(q^Tv=0\); velocity must be tangent to the circle. Differentiating again gives \(v^Tv+q^Tg-\lambda L^2=0\), determining the multiplier \(\lambda=(v^Tv+q^Tg)/L^2\). The original position constraint contains no direct multiplier, so the index-one elimination used above does not apply.

At the bottom of a 1 m pendulum take \(q=(0,-1)\) m, \(v=(2,0)\) m/s and \(g=(0,-9.81)\) m/s². The multiplier is \(13.81\) s⁻² and acceleration is \((0,4)\) m/s², the required inward centripetal acceleration. A velocity with a nonzero vertical component would already violate tangency at this position. Advancing differentiated equations alone can let position and tangent constraints drift because rounding and discretisation accumulate. Check both \(q^Tq-L^2\) and \(q^Tv\) over time; projecting position alone may leave a velocity pointing slightly through the rod constraint.

An algebraic residual can hide an inaccurate state

For the linear example, change a consistent state at fixed time by \(\delta y=0.02,\ \delta z=-0.01\). Its constraint residual stays exactly zero, since those changes cancel in \(y+2z\). Its differential right-hand side changes by \(-0.03\). The perturbation lies along the constraint manifold and is invisible to any diagnostic that checks only the algebraic equation. Conversely a perturbation normal to the manifold can be easy to see in the residual even if \(y\) is accurate. Estimate time-discretisation error by a separate comparison, such as refining the time step at a common physical output time.

Project an inconsistent state in a physically weighted metric

Suppose linear constraints require \(Bq=g\), while an unconstrained time step produces \(q^\ast\) with a small violation. A correction can be selected by minimising \((q-q^\ast)^TM(q-q^\ast)/2\) subject to the constraint, with \(M\) symmetric positive definite and \(B\) of full row rank. Stationarity gives

\[M(q-q^\ast)+B^T\lambda=0.\]

Inserting the resulting \(q=q^\ast-M^{-1}B^T\lambda\) into the constraint yields the smaller system \(BM^{-1}B^T\lambda=Bq^\ast-g\). This formula corrects exactly the component normal to the feasible plane in the chosen metric.

If \(M\) is a mass matrix for velocity variables, minimising this norm has a kinetic-energy interpretation. For position variables it is still a chosen distance measure and should be justified by the desired correction. Dependent constraint rows can make the multiplier system singular even when the geometrically projected state is unique. Nonlinear constraints require updating their Jacobian during a projection, and a large correction may find a different feasible branch. Satisfying the algebraic constraint alone does not guarantee a time-accurate differential state.

Position and velocity require two distinct projections

Let \(M=\operatorname{diag}(1,4)\), \(B=(1,1)\) and require \(q_1+q_2=2\). An unconstrained update gives

\[q^\ast=(1.2,1.0)^T,\]

with gap 0.2. The scalar multiplier matrix is \(1+1/4=1.25\), so \(\lambda=0.16\). Since \(M^{-1}B^T=(1,1/4)^T\), the correction is \((-0.16,-0.04)^T\), leaving \(q=(1.04,0.96)^T\). A Euclidean projection would subtract 0.1 from each coordinate. Both restore the same constraint, but the mass-weighted correction moves the heavier coordinate less.

For a constant constraint value, consistent velocities must also satisfy \(Bv=0\). Suppose \(v^\ast=(2,-1)^T\), with violation one. The velocity multiplier is \(1/1.25=0.8\), giving \(v=(1.2,-1.2)^T\). Its kinetic energy is \((1.2^2+4\cdot1.2^2)/2=3.6\), compared with \((2^2+4)/2=4\) before correction. The lost 0.4 is exactly half the squared mass norm of the removed normal component \((0.8,0.2)^T\). Orthogonality eliminates the cross term in this energy decomposition.

This reduction illustrates why projecting velocities can suppress energy even if the underlying unconstrained update had no dissipative force. In a simulation of sustained motion, report correction norms or constraint impulses along with the residual after projection. With a moving constraint \(Bq=g(t)\), the velocity condition becomes \(Bv=g'(t)\), so forced boundary motion can do physical work. Applying a zero-velocity projection to that case would remove a legitimate component of the solution.

For the linear position example the chosen correction is also the smallest in the prescribed metric by direct comparison. Every other feasible correction has the form \((-0.16,-0.04)+t(1,-1)\). Its squared mass norm equals \(0.032+5t^2\) because the mass-weighted cross term vanishes. The Euclidean correction corresponds to \(t=0.06\), giving squared mass norm 0.05 rather than 0.032. This calculation makes the meaning of the multiplier solution visible without invoking a general optimisation theorem. If \(B\) varies with time, differentiating \(B(t)q=g(t)\) also introduces the term \(B'(t)q\) in the velocity condition, even for a constant prescribed value.

The derivative of the constraint must therefore be evaluated at the same time as the corrected velocities.

Problem sheet 6 · Boundary and algebraic conditions

Recover missing initial data, measure shooting amplification, and calculate a consistent implicit step for a constrained state.

A slope from an endpoint

Solve \(y''=2\), \(y(0)=1,\ y(2)=3\) by shooting. Write the endpoint residual and derivative, take a Newton update from zero, and evaluate the fitted midpoint value.

Worked solution

With initial slope \(s\), \(y(t)=1+st+t^2\). The residual is

\[1+2s+4-3=2+2s.\]

Its derivative is 2.

The update from zero gives \(s=-1\) exactly because the residual is affine. At \(t=1\) the fitted value is 1; at \(t=2\) it returns 3. The trajectory initially decreases before turning upward.

Quantify an unstable direction

For \(y''=16y,\ y(0)=0\), express \(y(1)\) as a function of the initial slope. Calculate the slope needed for \(y(1)=2\), and estimate the change in terminal value caused by an absolute slope error of 0.001.

Worked solution

Superposition gives

\[y(t)=(s/4)\sinh(4t).\]

Hence \(s=8/\sinh4\approx0.29315\) and the endpoint sensitivity is \(\sinh4/4\approx6.8225\).

A slope error of 0.001 causes terminal error about 0.00682. This is exact up to rounding in the multiplier because the differential equation and its slope dependence are linear.

Enforce a time-dependent constraint

For \(y'=z,\ y+z=1+t,\ y(0)=0\), find consistent initial values and one backward Euler step of length 0.5. Solve the reduced equation and assess why the step has this particular error.

Worked solution

The initial constraint gives \(z(0)=1\) and therefore \(y'(0)=1\). At the new time \(y_1=0.5z_1\), \(y_1+z_1=1.5\), yielding \(y_1=0.5,z_1=1\).

The reduced equation \(y'=1+t-y\) has exact solution \(y=t\) with this initial value. Its derivative is constant along the trajectory, so backward Euler reproduces it exactly here. Different initial data introduce an exponential transient for which the step would have time-discretisation error.

Separate conservation, stability and accuracy in a decay calculation

A transfers to B with rate 0.8 s⁻¹ and A+B = 1 mol/m³. Start from pure A. Use Euler with a 1.5 s step, backward Euler with the same step, and the exact solution to find A and B after one step. Which tests pass? Predict the fine-grid error ratio for RK4 in the downloadable run, and identify a meaningful comparison of computational effort.

Worked solution

The exact A concentration is exp(−1.2) ≈ 0.3010, leaving B ≈ 0.6990. Forward Euler multiplies A by 1−1.2 = −0.2, giving A = −0.2 and B = 1.2. Total concentration is conserved. The amplification modulus is less than one, so repeated steps decay, but every other A value is negative. Absolute stability and positivity test different properties.

Backward Euler gives A = 1/(1+1.2) ≈ 0.4545 and B ≈ 0.5455. Both are positive and their sum is one, yet the A error is about 0.1535. These invariant checks detect important failures, but their success cannot quantify time-discretisation error.

For a smooth solution on a fixed interval the global RK4 error should fall by approximately 2⁴ when the step is halved in its asymptotic range. Use the chapter 2 OneSimulation plot to estimate the order from the last two mesh sizes. For a cost comparison count four right-hand-side calls per RK4 step versus one per Euler step. This model is deliberately small enough that wall-clock startup time would obscure that comparison.

Chapter 13 · Implicit midpoint, reversibility and the phase of an oscillator

Imagine integrating a mechanical trajectory forward, saving only its end state and then running the same method backward. Recovering the start is a stronger demand than obtaining a plausibly small one-step error. Even exact recovery does not guarantee that an oscillation peaked at the correct time.

Build a method from a centred difference

Integrate \(y'=f(t,y)\) over an interval of length \(h\). Midpoint quadrature approximates the integral by \(h\) times its integrand at \(t_n+h/2\). The arithmetic mean of the unknown endpoint states approximates the exact midpoint state up to order \(h^2\) on a smooth trajectory. Using this mean as the derivative's state argument gives

\[y_{n+1}-y_n=h f\!\left(t_n+h/2,(y_n+y_{n+1})/2\right).\]

Errors from midpoint quadrature and from evaluating \(f\) at the approximate midpoint both contribute \(O(h^3)\) locally, giving global order two on a fixed interval under the usual regularity and stability conditions. In general the unknown appears inside \(f\), so an implicit equation must be solved. If \(f\) is Lipschitz with constant \(L_f\), direct fixed-point iteration contracts when \(|h|L_f/2<1\). Newton iteration can converge over a wider range. Neither a failed nonlinear solve nor loose tolerances inherit the exact algebraic properties of the method.

Exchange \(y_n,y_{n+1}\) and replace \(h\) by \(-h\). The left side changes sign, the arithmetic midpoint is identical and the midpoint time is identical if the reverse step starts at \(t_{n+1}\). Thus, on the same locally unique solution branch,

\[\Phi_{-h}=\Phi_h^{-1}.\]

A round trip using variable steps must repeat the accepted steps in reverse order. Independently choosing a new adaptive mesh on the way back need not retrace the states.

Solve the step for a harmonic oscillator

Write \(q'=\omega p,\ p'=-\omega q\), with scaled momentum \(p\) in the same units as \(q\), and \(z=q+ip\). The ODE becomes

\[z'=-i\omega z.\]

Solving the linear midpoint equation gives the scalar multiplier

\[M(u)=\frac{1-iu/2}{1+iu/2}=\exp[-2i\arctan(u/2)],\]
\[u=\omega h.\]

Its numerator and denominator are complex conjugates. Hence it preserves \(q^2+p^2\), proportional to the oscillator energy, exactly in real arithmetic. For any radius or step size \(M(-u)M(u)=1\). The rotation per step is

\[\theta=2\arctan(u/2)=u-u^3/12+O(u^5).\]

The numerical phase lags by \(u-\theta\) per step. After \(N\) steps the accumulated lag is \(N(u-\theta)\), increasing linearly in elapsed time even while the energy remains exactly constant.

Compare explicit midpoint Runge–Kutta. A half Euler predictor leads to

\[M_{\rm RK2}=1-iu-u^2/2.\]

It also has order two, but \(|M_{\rm RK2}|^2=1+u^4/4\). The amplitude grows slowly at every nonzero step. A forward and reverse pair multiplies the starting amplitude by \(1+u^4/4\). In a conservative problem these two second-order procedures can produce visibly different long records.

Inspect the return and the lag separately

With \(\omega=1\), \(h=0.5\), \(N=20\), exact accumulation is 10 radians. Midpoint gives \(40\arctan(0.25)=9.7991\) radians, a lag of \(0.2009\) radians. The explicit round trip multiplies the radius by \((1+0.5^4/4)^{20}\simeq1.364\). The implicit round trip returns the original radius and phase to roundoff. Run both updates below. Compare results at equal elapsed time by halving \(h\) and doubling \(N\). The phase error should drop by about a factor of four. The graph uses computed steps for the methods and a more finely sampled exact reference.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on designing an accuracy criterion

A solver preserves the harmonic energy exactly over a simulation of 100 time units with \(\omega=1\). A measurement also requires phase error below 0.01 radians. Use the leading midpoint error to estimate an admissible step. How would you verify it?

Work through the solution

The accumulated phase error is approximately \(T\omega^3h^2/12\). Solving \(100h^2/12<0.01\) gives \(h<0.0346\). Verify at a chosen integer step count using \(T-2N\arctan[T/(2N)]\), or by convergence against a refined trajectory that measures the phase continuously. Energy alone has zero error for all these steps, so it cannot certify the phase target.

Further reading · Hairer's TUM geometric numerical integration course, symmetric methods.

Chapter 14 · Splitting a stiff relaxation from a slowly varying drive

A sensor tries to follow a changing reference while a fast relaxation damps departures. An explicit method may oscillate wildly even though the underlying transient decays monotonically. Treating the restoring term implicitly can resolve the slow motion using steps longer than the transient time. Whether those steps measure the transient accurately is a different question.

Construct an exact benchmark with two time scales

Let \(s(t)=\sin t\) and choose a relaxation time \(\varepsilon>0\). Consider \(y'=s'(t)+(s(t)-y)/\varepsilon\), with \(y(0)=1\). Set \(e=y-s\). Subtraction of \(s'\) gives \(e'=-e/\varepsilon\), hence the reference solution \(y(t)=\sin t+e^{-t/\varepsilon}\). The slow reference changes over times of order one, while the initial mismatch decays significantly over \(\varepsilon\). We know both pieces exactly and can diagnose errors even when a stable result looks visually smooth.

Explicit Euler applied to a perturbation of the state multiplies that perturbation by \(1-r\), with \(r=h/\varepsilon\). It damps monotonically only for \(0<r\le1\), changes sign while damping for \(1<r<2\), and grows for \(r>2\). These statements compare nearby solutions at equal drive. The full signal also contains the time variation of \(s\). At the boundary \(r=2\) a state perturbation alternates without decaying. Refining the step to follow a slow input alone can therefore give the wrong choice of \(h\).

Solve only the fast part implicitly

Split \(f_{\rm slow}(t)=\cos t\) from \(f_{\rm fast}(t,y)=(\sin t-y)/\varepsilon\). Evaluate the slow part at \(t_n\) and the fast part at \(t_{n+1},y_{n+1}\). This first-order additive implicit-explicit Euler step needs one scalar linear solve:

\[(1+r)y_{n+1}=y_n+h\cos t_n+r\sin t_{n+1}.\]

The state perturbation now multiplies by \(1/(1+r)\). It is damped at every positive step and tends to zero as \(r\) grows. Subtract \(\sin t_{n+1}\) from both sides after solving. The mismatch obeys \(e_{n+1}=[e_n+h\cos t_n-(\sin t_{n+1}-\sin t_n)]/(1+r)\). The bracketed forcing defect is \(O(h^2)\) for smooth input. In the stiff limit at fixed \(h\), the endpoint approaches the current reference. Moving the reference in the fast term to the old time would instead drag it one sample behind in that limit. The choice of what to treat implicitly includes the time at which a fast equilibrium is imposed.

Test early-time accuracy and late-time stability

For \(\varepsilon=0.02\), \(h=0.05\), \(r=2.5\). At the first step the exact value is

\[\sin(0.05)+e^{-2.5}=0.1321.\]

Explicit Euler gives

\[1+0.05(1-50)=-1.45.\]

The implicit-explicit value is

\[[1+0.05+2.5\sin(0.05)]/3.5=0.3357.\]

The latter is stable but visibly overestimates the surviving transient. Its damping factor \(1/3.5=0.286\) differs substantially from \(e^{-2.5}=0.0821\). A question about peak rate or initial settling requires shorter steps even if the late-time reference is recovered.

The experiment holds the final time at one while the number of steps changes. The vertical coordinate is the base-ten logarithm of absolute error, with values below \(10^{-15}\) drawn at that floor. Negative heights therefore correspond to errors less than one. Track the maximum error separately from the final error as the transient dies out. Try an initially unstable explicit run, then double the number of steps. The stiffness criterion depends on the ratio \(h/\varepsilon\), while accuracy on the reference also depends on \(h\) itself.

Tutorial on identifying which component is stiff

Keep \(h=0.04\) and reduce \(\varepsilon\) from 0.04 to 0.004. For each case compare exact, explicit and implicit amplification of a state perturbation. What quantity would force a reduction of \(h\) even with the implicit-explicit method?

Work through the solution

At \(r=1\) the three factors are \(e^{-1}=0.368\), zero and \(1/2\). At \(r=10\) they are \(4.54\times10^{-5}\), \(-9\) and \(1/11=0.0909\). For a measurement of the initial decay rate the implicit discretisation substantially misrepresents both exact factors. Bounding error during that transient requires smaller steps relative to \(\varepsilon\), even though its numerical amplification is stable.

Chapter 15 · Why a boundary value problem can be easier than its shooting equation

A concentration decays across a slab, with concentrations known at both faces. Computing a solution from the first face alone can bring in an exponentially growing mode. Enforcing both faces together gives a different numerical problem with the same differential equation.

Distinguish the boundary map from the physical solution

Use a nondimensional interval \(0\le x\le1\) and write \(y''=\lambda^2y\), with \(y(0)=1\), \(y(1)=e^{-\lambda}\), \(\lambda>0\). The exact solution is \(e^{-\lambda x}\), a smooth layer of thickness approximately \(1/\lambda\). A shooting method prescribes a tentative slope \(s=y'(0)\), integrates the initial value problem, and adjusts \(s\) to meet the second boundary. The exact initial-value solution is \(\cosh(\lambda x)+(s/\lambda)\sinh(\lambda x)\). The correct slope is \(-\lambda\). The small right boundary is recovered by subtracting two large nearly equal terms.

Perturb the exact slope by \(\delta s\). Superposition gives the resulting error everywhere without a second initial-value solve:

\[\delta y(x)=\frac{\delta s}{\lambda}\sinh(\lambda x),\]
\[\frac{d\,y(1)}{ds}=\frac{\sinh\lambda}{\lambda}.\]

At \(\lambda=20\) the right-end amplification is \(1.21\times10^7\). A slope error as small as \(10^{-12}\) gives about \(1.21\times10^{-5}\) endpoint error, while the target value is just \(2.06\times10^{-9}\). The actual boundary problem is much less sensitive to changes in prescribed concentrations. Its basis functions for arbitrary face values are \(\sinh[\lambda(1-x)]/\sinh\lambda\) and \(\sinh(\lambda x)/\sinh\lambda\), each bounded by one. A stable physical boundary problem can be paired with an ill-conditioned forward initial-value calculation. The particular derivative above measures sensitivity of the forward shooting evaluation.

Enforce the equation at the interior nodes

Take \(N\) equal intervals with \(h=1/N\). The centred second difference of any smooth exact solution equals \(y''(x_j)+h^2y^{(4)}(x_j)/12+O(h^4)\), by Taylor expansion about \(x_j\). Set that second difference equal to \(\lambda^2Y_j\). Incorporate the known boundary data \(Y_0,Y_N\) into the first and last right-hand sides. The interior equations are

\[-Y_{j-1}+[2+(\lambda h)^2]Y_j-Y_{j+1}=0,\]
\[j=1,\ldots,N-1.\]

Each row now couples neighbouring nodes and no row asks for a missing starting slope. The diagonal exceeds the sum of the off-diagonal magnitudes for \(\lambda>0\). Eliminate the lower diagonal from left to right and substitute back from the right. Both work and storage grow linearly with \(N\). This finite-difference collocation approximates the differential equation at grid points. It still needs enough points inside the layer. The grid parameter \(\lambda h\) measures its resolution.

At \(\lambda=5\) and just two intervals there is one unknown midpoint. Its equation gives \(Y_1=(1+e^{-5})/(2+2.5^2)=0.1220\), while the exact midpoint is

\[e^{-2.5}=0.08208.\]

Both boundary values are satisfied exactly. Their residual alone cannot reveal the interior error. As \(h\) is halved at fixed \(\lambda\), a well-resolved smooth solution exhibits second-order convergence.

Compare errors with a common physical target

The graph plots logarithmic absolute errors against position. The shooting curve isolates propagation of a controlled absolute initial slope perturbation of \(10^{-12}\), using the analytical propagation factor to avoid cancellation in the demonstration. The collocation curve solves the tridiagonal system numerically and compares each node to \(e^{-\lambda x_j}\). Values below \(10^{-16}\) are plotted at a floor. These are different sources of error. Increasing \(N\) improves collocation truncation error but cannot repair an error in the supplied shooting slope. At low \(\lambda\) the perturbed shooting solution may be much more accurate. Increase \(\lambda\) while keeping \(N\) fixed and identify where each method struggles.

Tutorial on a boundary tolerance

At \(\lambda=15\), require the error caused by the shooting slope alone to be less than \(10^{-8}\) at the right face. What absolute accuracy is needed in \(s\)? Give an independent accuracy check for the collocation solution.

Work through the solution

Rearrangement gives

\[|\delta s|<15\times10^{-8}/\sinh15=9.18\times10^{-14}.\]

For collocation solve on a nested mesh with half the spacing and compare shared nodes. In the second-order regime the refined error is roughly one third of the coarse-to-fine difference there. Check that the estimate stabilises under another refinement and that the fine mesh resolves \(1/\lambda\).

Chapter 16 · How two decaying modes produce a growing signal

Solve a triangular system exactly, derive the energy growth criterion and separate a real transient from numerical instability.

A coupling hidden by the eigenvalues

Let \(y_1'=-y_1+K y_2,\ y_2'=-2y_2\), with time in scaled units. The eigenvalues are −1 and −2 for every \(K\). Their negative real parts guarantee asymptotic decay of this system. They do not say that \(\|y(t)\|\) must decay monotonically. The second component acts as a temporary source in the first component before being damped away.

With initial state \((0,1)\), \(y_2=e^{-2t}\). Multiply the first equation by \(e^t\) and integrate from zero to \(t\). The result is

\[y_1=K(e^{-t}-e^{-2t}).\]

The largest absolute excursion of the first component occurs when \(2e^{-2t}=e^{-t}\), giving \(t=\log2\) and \(y_1=K/4\). At \(K=12\), a unit initial state generates a measured first component of 3.

Which initial directions grow at the initial instant?

Write \(E=\|y\|_2^2/2\). Differentiation gives

\[E'=y^T S y,\]

with \(S=(A+A^T)/2\). Here \(S\) has diagonal \((-1,-2)\) and off-diagonal \(K/2\). A positive eigenvalue appears when the determinant \(2-K^2/4\) becomes negative. Thus some direction has positive instantaneous energy growth for \(|K|>\sqrt8\). The specific initial vector above nevertheless has \(E'(0)=-2\). Existence of a growing direction and immediate growth of every chosen initial condition are separate statements.

A numerical check with a false lead

Set \(K=8\). At \(t=\log2\) the exact components are \(2,1/4\). Forward Euler with a sufficiently small step will show an initial dip followed by a rise in the norm. Refining time steps changes the approximation to the peak but does not eliminate the physical growth. By contrast, using a coarse Euler step \(h>1\) puts the scalar mode −2 outside the forward-Euler stability interval and introduces genuine numerical growth.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

The experiment plots the analytic components and norm. Use the signed coupling to reverse the first component. Explain why the norm is invariant under that sign change, and why a successful eigenvalue stability test alone cannot certify a bound on a short-time output.

Tutorial on transient amplification

Can a normal matrix with only negative real eigenvalues produce this Euclidean-norm amplification?

Worked solution

A normal matrix is unitarily diagonalizable. Its evolution is a unitary change of independent decaying factors, giving a norm at most one for \(t\ge0\). Nonorthogonality of the damped directions matters here.

Change the initial state to \((1,1)\) at \(K=6\). What is the initial energy slope?

Worked solution
\[E'=-y_1^2-2y_2^2+K y_1y_2=-1-2+6=3.\]

The instrumental first-component peak and the maximum total Euclidean norm generally occur at different times.

Chapter 17 · Locating a grazing event between time steps

Derive a sensitivity formula for event time, then measure how a harmless error in a trajectory can create or remove two threshold crossings.

A time is an implicitly defined output

Suppose an integrator advances \(y'=f(t,y)\) and an event occurs at \(g(t,y(t))=0\). Along the solution the derivative of the event function is \(\dot g=g_t+\nabla_y g\cdot f\). A perturbation \(\delta g\) in the computed event function shifts a simple event time by \(\delta t\simeq-\delta g/\dot g\). This estimate separates trajectory accuracy from event-time accuracy. At a tangency the denominator vanishes and the formula cannot be used.

Background · Event transversality
\[\dot g=\partial_tg+\nabla g\cdot f\]

For a simple event this directional derivative is nonzero. Near grazing, a small state error can create a much larger error in the event time.

Resolve two crossings analytically

Take \(g(t)=(t-t_0)^2-d\), a local model for a turning point relative to a sensor threshold. For \(d>0\) the events are \(t_0\pm\sqrt d\). Their separation is \(2\sqrt d\), and their slopes are \(\pm2\sqrt d\). For \(d=0\) there is only a tangent touch. If \(d<0\), the sensor is never triggered. An additive constant perturbation \(\epsilon\) changes the effective parameter from \(d\) to \(d-\epsilon\).

At \(t_0=1\), \(d=0.01\) and \(\epsilon=0.001\), the first event moves from 0.9 to about 0.90513. The linear prediction is

\[0.001/0.2=0.005.\]

At \(d=0.0004\) the same perturbation removes both crossings entirely. Reporting more digits from a polished scalar rootfinder would not recover them.

What two endpoint samples fail to tell you

With \(d=0.0025\), a step from 0.9 to 1.1 has equal positive endpoint values of 0.0075 but crosses the threshold twice. Dense interpolation followed by an extremum check can detect this pair if the interpolant resolves the turning point. A sign-change test at accepted endpoints alone cannot. An interpolant is itself an approximation, so event feasibility should be tested against an error scale at the extremum.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on event tolerances

For a simple crossing with slope magnitude 0.04 units/s, what error in the event function is consistent with a 1 ms time tolerance to first order?

Worked solution

Multiply the slope by the desired time uncertainty. The event function should be accurate to about \(4\times10^{-5}\) units near the crossing.

A contact model needs the first event, even if it only touches the threshold and returns. How should the experiment be reported near grazing?

Worked solution

Report the minimum event-function value and an estimated error on it, in addition to any time estimates. If zero lies within that uncertainty one cannot distinguish no crossing, tangency and a nearby pair from these data alone.

Chapter 18 · A constrained integrator can get the circle right and the clock wrong

A frictionless bead moves on an ideal circular guide. Its exact path, speed and angular phase can all be calculated by hand. Use this unusually informative reference to derive RATTLE's two constraint corrections and see which numerical errors would escape a camera that measured position alone.

Recover the force exerted by a perfectly smooth guide

Use a circle of radius one metre, and measure mass in units of the bead mass. The position \(q=(x,y)\) and velocity \(v\) are expressed in Cartesian coordinates. The guide exerts a reaction normal to its surface and no tangential force. Writing this reaction per unit mass as a scalar multiplier times \(q\) gives

\[\dot q=v,\]
\[\dot v=-\lambda q.\]

The sign convention makes positive \(\lambda\) point inward. The equation \(q\cdot q=1\) fixes the position but does not by itself describe which velocities are physically admissible.

Differentiate the constraint once to find

\[q\cdot v=0.\]

Differentiate again to obtain

\[v\cdot v+q\cdot\dot v=0.\]

Substituting the force yields

\[\lambda=(v\cdot v)/(q\cdot q).\]

Thus the required inward acceleration grows as the square of speed. The power supplied by the guide is \(-\lambda q\cdot v=0\), so the speed stays constant. The angular momentum per unit mass \(xv_y-yv_x\) also stays constant because the guide force passes through the centre. These are independent diagnostics one can compute directly from a numerical trajectory.

Start at \(q=(1,0)\) with \(v=(0,\omega)\). The exact position is \((\cos\omega t,\sin\omega t)\), the exact velocity is its derivative, and the exact angular momentum is \(\omega\) in the unit-radius convention. An angle coordinate would make this example elementary. The point of the exercise is to keep Cartesian variables, as would be necessary for many connected bodies whose constraints cannot conveniently be eliminated in one global chart.

Solve for the first constraint impulse

Think of a velocity-Verlet step as an initial impulse, a drift, and a final impulse. With no external force the initial impulse is parallel to the old normal \(q_n\). Absorb its unknown magnitude and factors of time into a dimensionless coefficient \(a\). After the drift the candidate position must have the form \(q_{n+1}=a q_n+h v_n\). Since \(q_n\) has unit length and \(v_n\) is tangent, the norm of this expression has no cross term. Imposing the new position constraint gives a scalar quadratic equation and two possible roots.

\[a^2+h^2\|v_n\|^2=1,\]
\[a=\sqrt{1-h^2\|v_n\|^2}.\]

Choose the positive solution because the computed point must approach the old one as \(h\) goes to zero. The negative root approaches the opposite point on the circle. Its algebraic admissibility is not evidence that it represents the same initial-value problem. When \(h\|v_n\|>1\) the construction has no real solution at all. This is a geometric limit on the step, distinct from an estimate of integration accuracy. Even before reaching the limit the step can introduce a substantial angular error.

The velocity used during the drift is the chord velocity \(v_{n+1/2}=(q_{n+1}-q_n)/h\). It contains a small inward term \((a-1)q_n/h\). Expanding the square root yields

\[a=1-h^2\|v_n\|^2/2+O(h^4).\]

This shows how a half-step of centripetal acceleration emerges from the constraint impulse. There was no need to insert the exact circular trajectory to obtain it.

Recover a tangent velocity at the new point

Although both ends of a chord lie on the guide, a chord velocity is generally not tangent at its destination. Apply the second normal impulse along \(q_{n+1}\). To eliminate the normal component subtract its scalar projection, using the fact that the new normal again has unit length. This gives the velocity form of the second constraint correction.

\[v_{n+1}=v_{n+1/2}-(q_{n+1}\cdot v_{n+1/2})q_{n+1}.\]

The two impulses preserve angular momentum about the origin. The old-normal impulse makes no torque about the old position. The straight drift preserves the cross product of chord velocity with position because adding a parallel displacement does not change that cross product. The final impulse makes no torque about the new position. In this special problem tangency, unit radius and preserved angular momentum together also preserve speed. For a more general constrained Hamiltonian system, exact preservation of kinetic energy does not follow from the same argument.

Obtain the phase change directly from the new position. Its component along the old unit tangent is \(h\omega\); its component along the old radius is \(a\). For the branch chosen here the step advances the phase by \(\arcsin(h\omega)\). The exact time increment advances by \(h\omega\). Their difference is \(h^3\omega^3/6+O(h^5)\) for a small step. After \(T/h\) steps at fixed final time the total leading phase error is \(T h^2\omega^3/6\). This is a precise second-order error prediction for an observable that energy and radius cannot diagnose.

A comparison with a plausible geometric shortcut

Another algorithm proposes an unconstrained drift \(q_n+h v_n\), rescales this position to the circle and then projects the old velocity onto the new tangent. Under this rule the angle change per step is \(\arctan(h\|v_n\|)\) and the tangential speed changes by a factor \(1/\sqrt{1+h^2\|v_n\|^2}\). One finds the first expression by comparing the radial and tangential components before normalisation. The second follows by taking the dot product of the two successive unit tangents.

Thus this alternative delivers perfect radii and tangent velocities while steadily slowing the bead in a frictionless apparatus. Taking reciprocals of the squared-speed relation even gives

\[1/s_{n+1}^2=1/s_n^2+h^2.\]

After many steps its speed can be predicted without running a single time loop. This is a useful second independent reference for the notebook. A method that projects state components separately need not realise the constraint force and its work correctly.

With the default \(\omega=1.6\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}\) and \(h=0.25\) s, the product \(h\omega\) is 0.4. The constrained step therefore advances by 0.411517 radians instead of 0.4. After 80 steps its lead is about 0.92135 radians. The analytical speed, radius and angular momentum are still reproduced to roundoff. This example makes the choice of reported output a scientific decision. A machine timed by successive crossings of a sensor would give a wrong timing signal even though a ruler would see the correct orbit radius.

Check an order of accuracy without changing the time horizon

Double the number of steps while halving their duration to retain the same final time. For the default run the lead drops from about 0.92135 to about 0.21727 radians. The quotient is a little more than four because the larger step is still influenced by higher powers of \(h\omega\). Keep refining until the quotient settles near four. If instead the step were halved with the number of steps fixed, the run would end earlier and both the elapsed time and the error per step would change. Such a comparison cannot isolate the global order at fixed final time.

An angle reported by an inverse tangent jumps from positive to negative pi across its branch cut. Differentiate or subtract such wrapped angles without care and the resulting spurious jumps can dominate a phase-error graph. Here each step moves through less than a quarter circle, so consecutive angle differences are unambiguous under phase unwrapping. Sampling a more rapidly moving system less often could reintroduce an ambiguity even with an accurate integrator internally.

A local OneSimulation installation permits another independent comparison. The optional notebook cell supplies its classical four-stage Runge–Kutta method with the continuous Cartesian acceleration obtained above. This method has no algebraic position correction; comparing its end radius and angular timing helps to distinguish the order of a differential equation solver from the constraint properties of the discrete update. For a distant endpoint one should test each diagnostic over the whole interval and repeat the comparison at a smaller step.

An additional time-reversal test can be made without knowing the exact phase. Integrate forward for a chosen number of steps, negate the final velocity and integrate the same number of positive steps. For the exact constrained update in this free problem, each second step sequence undoes the corresponding first rotation on the same branch. The final position returns to its initial value and the terminal velocity is the negative initial one. A return-to-origin test can therefore pass even when the intermediate phase differs visibly from the true physical clock. This is why a reversibility check complements, rather than replaces, a measurement at a specified observation time.

Tutorial on enforcing a shape and measuring a time

1. Start at unit radius with tangential speed 1 m/s. Use a half-second constrained step to calculate the new position and the discrete phase advance.

Work through the solution

In the chosen coordinates the new point is \((\sqrt{3}/2,1/2)\). Its polar angle is \(\pi/6=0.52360\) radians. The exact solution at half a second has angle 0.5 radians. A single step is already ahead by approximately 0.02360 radians.

2. For the same initial data and step, find the speed after four separately normalised drifts. What would a kinetic-energy sensor read?

Work through the solution

The reciprocal squared speed starts at one and increases by 0.25 per step. After four steps the speed is \(1/\sqrt2\) m/s. With unit mass the reported energy is 0.25 J instead of the original 0.5 J. All four reported positions still lie on the circle.

3. A student proposes accepting a step whenever its radius error is below \(10^{-10}\) m. Supply a quantitative additional criterion for a phase error below 0.01 radians over ten seconds at \(\omega=1\).

Work through the solution

The leading estimate gives \(10h^2/6<0.01\), hence \(h<0.0775\) s approximately. Use the exact error \(10[\arcsin(h)-h]/h\) or a refinement comparison for the final check; the asymptotic bound alone is not a rigorous guarantee at its edge. Tangency and torque balance remain useful complementary diagnostics.

Further reading · Hans Andersen, the RATTLE velocity algorithm for constrained motion

Problem sheet 7 · Project · Choose a time integrator from measurements

A velocity sensor monitors an oscillator with one nearly conservative coordinate and one rapidly relaxing auxiliary variable. Design distinct numerical checks for phase, damping and the conditioning of a boundary measurement.

A stable calculation with the wrong period

The ideal oscillator has angular frequency \(2\) s⁻¹. Use an implicit midpoint step of 0.5 s. Derive the discrete rotation angle by acting on the complex scalar \(u'=i\omega u\). Estimate how far the numerical phase lags after 20 s. Compare with the midpoint experiment.

Work through the solution

One step multiplies by \((1+i\omega h/2)/(1-i\omega h/2)\). Its magnitude is one and argument \(2\arctan(0.5)=0.927295\) rad. In forty steps the exact phase advances 40 rad and the computed phase about 37.092 rad. The lag is about 2.91 rad even though the amplitude stays constant. A plot of energy alone cannot certify the period.

Relax a sensor without overdrawing the step budget

The auxiliary measurement follows \(z'=-(z-u)/\tau\), with \(\tau=0.02\) s. During one short window, regard \(u=1\) as constant and start from \(z=0\). Compare forward and backward Euler and the exact value at \(h=0.1\) s. Which qualitative property matters when the measurement represents a bounded response?

Work through the solution

Forward Euler gives \(z_1=5\). Backward Euler gives \(z_1=(z_0+5u)/(1+5)=5/6\). The exact value is \(1-e^{-5}\approx0.99326\). The implicit step is bounded and monotone but its transient error is substantial. Use the split relaxation experiment to distinguish resolving a transient from staying stable after it.

Diagnose a spatial boundary sensor

A separate steady calibration solves \(y''=k^2 y\) on \([0,1]\) with specified endpoint values. Starting a shooting solution with slope error \(\delta s\) at zero and exact starting value, derive the right-end error. Evaluate it at \(k=10\), \(\delta s=10^{-5}\). Why might a global boundary solve help?

Work through the solution

The error solves the homogeneous equation with \(e(0)=0\) and \(e'(0)=\delta s\). Therefore \(e(1)=\delta s\sinh(k)/k\approx0.01101\). A trial slope that looks accurate in isolation gives a visibly wrong right boundary. A collocation matrix imposes both boundaries together; its own error and conditioning still need verification. Compare the two boundary solvers.

Design a reporting table

Name a distinct scalar check for each part of this instrument. State when you would halve the time step and when you would change the mathematical formulation.

Work through the solution

Record accumulated phase at a fixed physical time, range and time-to-90% for the sensor response, and endpoint residual plus interior profile error for the spatial calibration. Halve the time step to test temporal truncation error. Change the boundary solver when initial-slope perturbations dominate the measurement despite a converged integrator.

Problem sheet 8 · Differentiate a bouncing trajectory through an impact

A camera records height at a fixed time after an object hits the floor. A nearby release height changes the impact time as well as the rebound speed. Compute the sensitivity that an estimator needs, and see why differentiating just the reset of velocity gives an incorrect answer even with an accurately located impact.

Distinguish a fixed observation time from an event time

A point mass falls from rest at height \(h\) under acceleration \(-g\). Let the rigid floor reset its incoming velocity \(v^-\) to \(v^+=-e v^-\), where \(0<e<1\). Derive its first impact time and the derivative of that time with respect to \(h\). Which derivative of height vanishes at the impact?

Work through the solution

Before contact \(y=h-gt^2/2\), \(v=-gt\). Setting \(y=0\) gives

\[\tau=\sqrt{2h/g},\]
\[d\tau/dh=1/(g\tau).\]

The height evaluated at its own impact is \(y(\tau(h),h)=0\) for every release, so its total derivative is zero. At a fixed time before impact the partial derivative \(\partial y/\partial h\) is instead one. The chain rule relates them: \(1+v^-d\tau/dh=0\), since \(v^-=-g\tau\). A camera clock held fixed during the experiment asks for a fixed-time sensitivity. A switch that records exactly at contact uses the event-time derivative. They describe different observations of the same trajectory.

Derive the missing timing term in the tangent update

Write \(X=(y,v)\), \(F=(v,-g)\), \(n=(1,0)^T\) and the reset \(R(y,v)=(y,-ev)\) on the guard \(y=0\). For a state perturbation \(z^-\) compared at the nominal event time, follow it to its own event and then synchronise it with the nominal post-impact time. What matrix maps it to \(z^+\)?

Work through the solution

The perturbed impact is shifted by \(\delta\tau=-n^Tz^-/(n^TF^-)\). At its own event the perturbation of the incoming state is \(z^-+F^-\delta\tau\). Applying the reset derivative gives \(DR(z^-+F^-\delta\tau)\). To compare states at a common time, advance the reset state back by \(\delta\tau\) using the outgoing field, subtracting \(F^+\delta\tau\). Thus \(z^+=[DR+(F^+-DRF^-)n^T/(n^TF^-)]z^-\). The bracket is the saltation matrix. It includes the change in impact time and depends on both vector fields. A transversal crossing, \(n^TF^-\ne0\), is required for this calculation.

With \(DR={\rm diag}(1,-e)\) and \(F^+=(-ev^-,-g)\), the matrix has first row \((-e,0)\) and second row \((-(1+e)g/v^-,-e)\). The height perturbation's sign immediately after the event can seem surprising. A higher release reaches the floor later; at the nominal impact the newly rebounding higher-release trajectory has had less time to rise. The saltation matrix compares neighbouring smooth branches extended to a common time. Exactly at the discontinuity of the velocity field the full fixed-time map can be nonsmooth; the one-sided propagated tangent on either side is what the matrix supplies.

Compare the tangent to the exact rebound trajectory

Set \(h=1.00\,{\rm m}\), \(g=9.81\,{\rm m/s^2}\), \(e=0.80\), and observe at \(T=0.600\,{\rm s}\). Compute the height and velocity sensitivity with respect to initial height in SI units. What would a reset-only implementation predict?

Work through the solution

The impact occurs at \(0.45152\,{\rm s}\), leaving \(\Delta=T-\tau=0.14848\,{\rm s}\) of rebound. The pre-impact tangent is \((1,0)\), so just afterwards it becomes \((-e,(1+e)/\tau)\). Free transport to \(T\) gives

\[\partial_h y=-e+(1+e)\Delta/\tau\approx-0.208,\]
\[\partial_h v=(1+e)/\tau\approx3.99\,{\rm s^{-1}}.\]

The units of the first derivative are metres per metre. A naive reset-only operation leaves \((1,0)\) unchanged and gives both the wrong sign of the position response and zero velocity response.

One can check the calculation without any tangent integration. The first-rebound solution is

\[y=e g\tau(T-\tau)-g(T-\tau)^2/2,\]
\[v=e g\tau-g(T-\tau).\]

Differentiate these expressions at fixed \(T\), remembering \(\tau\) depends on \(h\), to obtain the same two results. At a known \(T\) shortly after rebound, a measured height alone may decrease when the release height increases. After sufficient time on the same rebound branch this derivative turns positive again. An inverse problem built around a single image should check whether its measurement time has landed close to a zero of the sensitivity.

Verify the derivative without crossing an unreported event

Use the graph to compare a two-sided finite difference of full trajectories with the analytic tangent. Why does disagreement form a narrow interval around impact, and why does unlimited step reduction fail at a grazing crossing?

Work through the solution

Compute \(D_\eta=[y(T;h+\eta)-y(T;h-\eta)]/(2\eta)\) at the same observation time in both runs. Away from the event, both follow the same smooth sequence of modes and the difference tends to the analytic tangent as \(\eta\) decreases. Near impact one run can still be falling while the other is already rebounding. Its finite difference mixes two branches and rounds off the jump between one-sided derivatives. Narrowing the perturbation shrinks this region. At exactly the nonsmooth observation time a unique classical derivative need not exist. The central value there cannot be taken as evidence that the analytic tangent on either smooth side is wrong.

Start with a one-percent perturbation and reduce it while monitoring a time well after the first bounce and before the second. At sufficiently small perturbations event-location tolerance and cancellation in subtraction become visible in a floating-point solver. For a nearly tangent hit the denominator \(n^TF^-\) approaches zero, and small perturbations can remove or insert an event entirely. The event sequence and its transversality are part of a sensitivity report, just as step size and integrator order are part of a trajectory calculation.

Notebook practice · A constrained integrator can get the circle right and the clock wrong

Dynamics and numerical time integration

Turn a differential model into a time step. Track accuracy, stability and structural properties separately in a computable physical or mathematical example.

  1. Find a crossing between accepted time steps

    An integrator can approximate a trajectory accurately yet report a poor switch time. Construct a continuous interpolant, relate a state error to an event-time error and inspect what happens when a trajectory merely touches a threshold.

  2. A delayed feedback model needs a history, not one starting value

    A controller responds to the measured displacement one time delay ago. Construct its solution in successive intervals, track propagated derivative discontinuities and derive the point at which increasing delay can destabilise negative feedback.

  3. Solving a two-point boundary problem without guessing the start slope

    When endpoint conditions act on different sides of an interval, shooting can amplify a tiny initial error. Construct a cubic collocation system, test its continuous differential residual and quantify which boundary-value solution the residual actually certifies.

  4. Why stiff stability does not guarantee the expected order

    A differential equation is constructed to have a smooth, known solution and a rapidly damped error mode. Use it to distinguish stability of the homogeneous equation, tracking of a moving target and the effect of off-grid stage forcing.

  5. How far does an energy-preserving rotation lag behind?

    Integrate uniform circular motion by the implicit midpoint rule. The points stay exactly on the energy circle in exact arithmetic. Derive the angle advanced at each step and measure the accumulated timing error.

  6. From existence of a trajectory to the order of a time step

    Establish what an initial-value solver approximates, derive Runge–Kutta order conditions for a general smooth vector field and construct an alternative high-order step by extrapolation.

  7. Why consistent multistep formulas can fail under refinement

    Derive the root condition for constant steps, propagate a local defect to a fixed end time and isolate a growing parasitic mode with a controlled starting error.

  8. Why an accurate position can hide an inaccurate constraint force

    Derive an energy test for implicit Runge–Kutta stages, then recover missing derivatives in a constrained model and calculate the accuracy lost by a multiplier.

Dynamics and numerical time integration · 1. Find a crossing between accepted time steps

An integrator can approximate a trajectory accurately yet report a poor switch time. Construct a continuous interpolant, relate a state error to an event-time error and inspect what happens when a trajectory merely touches a threshold.

An event is a root of a composite function

Let \(y'=f(t,y)\) and let a sensor or change of dynamics be described by \(g(t,y)=0\). Along an exact trajectory the scalar event function is \(h(t)=g(t,y(t))\). A simple event at \(t_*\) satisfies

\[\dot h_*=g_t+g_yf\ne0.\]

If approximate trajectory or sensor evaluation introduces error \(\delta h\), linearisation around the crossing gives

\[\delta t\simeq-\delta h/\dot h_*.\]

A small state error can therefore imply a much larger time error if the crossing is nearly tangential. Reporting an event timestamp to machine precision after bisecting a low-order interpolant is not equivalent to knowing the physical crossing time that accurately.

Consider a dimensionless unit-frequency oscillator with \(y=\cos t,\ y'=-\sin t\) during the first half cycle. Its first falling crossing of threshold \(a\in(0,1)\) occurs at \(t_*=\arccos a\), with slope \(-\sqrt{1-a^2}\). At \(a=0.5\), a positive vertical interpolation error \(10^{-3}\) can delay the crossing by about \(0.00115\) time units. At \(a=0.9999\) the same linear estimate is about 0.0707, comparable with or larger than the event time itself; the simple linear local approximation then also fails. At precisely \(a=1\) the surface is tangent at the maximum and sign-change tests can miss it altogether.

Construct a state and slope interpolant on one accepted interval

Suppose samples of state and derivative are available at \(t_n,t_{n+1}=t_n+h\). Write \(s=(t-t_n)/h\) and choose a cubic \(p(s)\) matching \(p(0)=y_n,p(1)=y_{n+1},p_s(0)=hf_n,p_s(1)=hf_{n+1}\). Solving these four linear conditions gives

\[p=(2s^3-3s^2+1)y_n+(s^3-2s^2+s)hf_n+(-2s^3+3s^2)y_{n+1}+(s^3-s^2)hf_{n+1}.\]

For an exact scalar trajectory sufficiently smooth in time, the Hermite error is \(y(t)-p(t)=y^{(4)}(\xi)(t-t_n)^2(t-t_{n+1})^2/4!\), bounded by \(h^4\max|y^{(4)}|/384\). Linear interpolation instead has error bounded by \(h^2\max|y''|/8\). Combine either bound with a known lower bound on \(|\dot h|\) in a bracket to bound event-time error. In an actual ODE solve, \(y_n,f_n\) are numerical, so interpolation and integrated state errors both contribute. A high-order interpolant cannot repair incorrect endpoint data or sample a discontinuity assumed smooth.

Bracket on the physical trajectory and restart after a transition

At each accepted interval compare event values at both ends. For a simple crossing of the requested direction, solve \(g(t,p(t))=0\) inside that interval using a bracketed root finder. The bracket controls the algebraic root-finding error; independent time-step refinement measures trajectory error. If the dynamics change at the event, truncate the old step there, apply any specified reset \(y^+=R(t_*,y^-)\) and restart the integrator under the new right side. Propagating the old smooth interpolant through a change of dynamics can corrupt both state and subsequent event ordering.

If a surface stays exactly active over a finite interval, repeated detection at the same time can stall the integration. A model of contact, hysteresis or directional rearming may be physically appropriate, depending on what the event means. Merely nudging the time forward by an arbitrary tolerance can skip a nearby second event. Similarly two opposite crossings within one large accepted interval may yield equal endpoint signs. A maximum step guided by known physical timescales, supplementary extrema detection or an analytically enclosed event function can be needed. Accuracy control on the smooth state alone does not guarantee detection of every specified surface crossing.

Compare interpolation errors independently of the propagation error

The calculator uses exact cosine endpoint samples and their derivatives, then locates the first falling crossing on a uniform time grid by linear or cubic Hermite interpolation. The curves show error against step spacing on a logarithmic vertical scale. Their irregular minima occur when a grid point happens to coincide with the event. Look for the error envelope over several spacings. At threshold 0.5 the exact result is 1.047198 s. Moving the threshold changes both the crossing slope and its position relative to the sampled grid.

Open the live experiment

Distinguish uncertainty in the threshold from time discretisation

Two clocks can agree on an event and still report a poorly determined arrival time if the physical threshold is uncertain. For a calibrated level \(a\) the falling crossing solves \(\cos t_*=a\), so implicit differentiation gives

\[dt_*/da=-1/\sqrt{1-a^2}.\]

A level error of \(0.002\) near \(a=0.5\) produces a timing uncertainty of about \(0.00231\) s for the unit-frequency benchmark. At \(a=0.9\) the same device gives \(0.00459\) s. Close to the turning point the trace is almost horizontal. Reducing the root finder's tolerance cannot remove that physical loss in sensitivity.

If the numerical trajectory also has a known absolute state error bound \(\eta\), divide that bound by a lower bound on the crossing speed in the bracket. The result is a deterministic timing allowance. An independent random threshold error has a standard deviation and calls for a statistical interpretation. Adding those numbers in quadrature would need a probability model for both. Reporting the two separately preserves what the measurement and the solver actually established.

Tutorial · assess a reported event time

Get a crossing from just two samples

Use \(y=\cos t,\ a=0.5\) with samples at \(t=0.8,1.2\). Compute the linearly interpolated time and its error. Why does its sign follow from curvature?

Work through the solution

The endpoint values are 0.696707 and 0.362358; the fractional position is \((0.696707-0.5)/(0.696707-0.362358)=0.5883\), giving \(t_L\simeq1.0353\). It is about 0.0119 s early. Over this interval \(\cos t>0\), so \(y''=-\cos t<0\). A straight chord lies below the concave curve; the falling chord reaches the threshold before the true trajectory.

Bound a Hermite crossing-time error

On the same interval use \(|y^{(4)}|\le1,\ |\dot y|\ge\sin0.8\). Give a simple guaranteed bound assuming exact endpoints and exact solution of the interpolating cubic root.

Work through the solution

The state interpolation bound is

\[0.4^4/384=6.67\times10^{-5}.\]

Dividing by \(\sin0.8=0.7174\) gives \(9.29\times10^{-5}\) s. This uses monotonicity of the exact trajectory to convert a vertical defect at the computed root into a horizontal difference. It does not assume the cubic derivative equals the true derivative. Numerical endpoint error would need to be added in state units first.

Distinguish a tangency from a missed sign change

A gap sensor follows \(h(t)=(t-1)^2-\delta\). Compare event detection at \(\delta=0,10^{-4},-10^{-4}\) over the interval \([0.9,1.1]\).

Work through the solution

Both endpoints are positive in all three cases. At zero there is a repeated touching event. At positive \(\delta\) there are two true crossings at 0.99 and 1.01 with opposite directions. At negative \(\delta\) there is no root. Locating and evaluating the internal minimum distinguishes the cases when the uncertainty is below \(|\delta|\). A sign test at just the endpoints cannot determine any of these distinctions, however accurately it evaluates those endpoints.

Dynamics and numerical time integration · 2. A delayed feedback model needs a history, not one starting value

A controller responds to the measured displacement one time delay ago. Construct its solution in successive intervals, track propagated derivative discontinuities and derive the point at which increasing delay can destabilise negative feedback.

Specify the additional data an ordinary initial value omits

For \(t\ge0\) consider \(y'(t)=-a y(t-\tau)\) with positive constant gain \(a\) and delay \(\tau>0\). The derivative at the start depends on \(y(-\tau)\); an initial value \(y(0)\) alone is insufficient. Supply a history \(y(t)=\phi(t)\) on \([-\tau,0]\), continuous at zero for a continuous state. Two preparations sharing \(y(0)\) but different earlier values can immediately develop different slopes. This is why treating a delay as a new algebraic coefficient in an ODE solver without a history buffer changes the physical problem.

Take constant history \(\phi=1\). For \(0\le t\le\tau\), the delayed input is one and \(y=1-at\). For \(\tau\le t\le2\tau\), the derivative is \(-a[1-a(t-\tau)]\); integrate from \(t=\tau\) to get

\[y=1-at+a^2(t-\tau)^2/2.\]

On the next interval another integral gives

\[y=1-at+a^2(t-\tau)^2/2-a^3(t-2\tau)^3/6.\]

Each new interval solves an ordinary forced equation with known earlier right-side data. This is the method of steps. A smooth-looking initial history can nevertheless mismatch derivatives: here the left derivative at zero is zero while the equation supplies \(-a\) on the right.

Trace how a step mismatch propagates through the delay

At \(t=\tau\) continuity of \(y\) at zero makes \(y'\) continuous, but \(y''(t)=-a y'(t-\tau)\) inherits the earlier jump in \(y'\). At \(2\tau\) the discontinuity appears in the third derivative. High-order adaptive ODE error formulas assume smooth derivatives within an interval. Marking known breakpoints and integrating separately across the delay intervals avoids applying a high-degree interpolant through an unacknowledged loss of smoothness. With several delays, sums of delay lengths can generate additional event locations and their bookkeeping matters. A discontinuity in the physical forcing can also generate new fronts of reduced regularity.

Within a method-of-steps solver, values \(y(t-\tau)\) usually lie between stored previous mesh points. Dense output supplies their interpolation. Its error feeds directly into the next interval's right side and can limit global accuracy even when the ODE time-step rule has higher formal order. Maintain enough history to cover the largest delay and requested outputs, and preserve the known one-sided behaviour at breakpoints. If a trial step is longer than the delay its stages may refer to the current unknown interval; an algorithm must either constrain that step or solve the resulting within-step interpolation problem consistently. A generic lookup of the last stored value instead creates an unintended zero-order-held feedback law.

Background · Can smooth data prevent the first derivative jump?

A constant history \(\phi(t)=1\) has zero left derivative at the start. The equation \(y'=-a\,y(t-\tau)\) demands right derivative \(-a\), creating an immediate mismatch. A history with \(\phi(-\tau)=1\) and \(\phi'(0^-)=-a\) instead matches the first derivative. For example, choose \(\phi(t)=1-a(t+\tau)\) on the history interval.

Matching a first derivative does not guarantee a smooth second derivative. Differentiating the equation once shows that the next matching condition would be

\[\phi''(0^-)=-a\,\phi'(-\tau^+).\]

For the linear history, the two sides are zero and \(a^2\). The mismatch has moved up by one derivative. A high-order interpolant across the start may therefore still lose order even though the trajectory and its tangent look continuous.

Derive a delay-induced change of oscillatory stability

For a constant-coefficient retarded equation try \(y=e^{st}\). Substitution gives

\[s+a e^{-s\tau}=0.\]

Unlike a polynomial characteristic equation, this transcendental equation generally has infinitely many roots. A pair crosses the imaginary axis when \(s=i\omega\), giving \(a\cos(\omega\tau)=0,\ \omega-a\sin(\omega\tau)=0\). For positive \(\omega\), the first crossing is at \(\omega=a,\ a\tau=\pi/2\). With negligible delay the model behaves like the stable ODE \(y'=-ay\). The crossing indicates that negative feedback acting on old information can become destabilising; stability of the zero-delay ODE is no guarantee for arbitrary delay.

To check crossing direction, differentiate \(s+a e^{-s\tau}=0\) at constant \(a\), obtaining \(ds/d\tau=-s^2/(1+\tau s)\) after using the characteristic equation. At \(s=ia\) its real part is \(a^2/[1+(a\tau)^2]>0\). Increasing delay moves this pair into the unstable half-plane. A numerically observed sign-changing transient alone does not prove instability, as stable delay systems can oscillate while decaying. Compare an envelope over successive periods with the characteristic roots and refine both time discretisation and history interpolation. An unstable numerical discretisation can otherwise imitate a feedback-induced growth.

Compare a stored history with the method-of-steps solution

The delay is the time unit and the history on the preceding interval is one. The calculator integrates an explicit Euler update with an integer number of steps per delay so it can read the delayed state exactly at a stored grid index. The comparison curve evaluates the exact piecewise polynomial. Its kth contribution starts after k minus one delay intervals and has coefficient given by the kth power of minus the dimensionless feedback divided by k factorial. This follows by repeatedly integrating the previous interval’s polynomial, so it is independent of the Euler recurrence.

At feedback one, inspect the exact values after one, two and three delays before refining the grid. Euler already integrates the constant derivative on the first interval exactly, while errors enter on later intervals. Change the feedback across π/2 and compare the oscillation envelope. Six displayed delays are useful for checking the calculation but can be too short to measure slow growth close to threshold. A stable-looking short record does not replace the characteristic-root analysis. Because the grids here align with the delay, this experiment isolates stepping error and does not test a general off-grid history interpolator.

Tutorial · distinguish history dependence from numerical error

Compute three exact method-of-steps values

For \(a=1\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\), \(\tau=1\) s and constant unit history, find \(y(1),y(2),y(3)\). Does a negative response at two seconds prove instability?

Work through the solution

The first value is zero. The second is

\[1-2+1/2=-0.5.\]

The third is

\[1-3+(3-1)^2/2-(3-2)^3/6=-1/6.\]

A negative value reflects delayed feedback overshoot. With \(a\tau=1<\pi/2\) the zero solution is asymptotically stable for this model even though the transient crosses zero. The exact interval polynomials provide stringent tests for a history interpolator at the first few breakpoints.

Construct incompatible histories with the same initial value

Keep \(a=\tau=1\) and compare \(\phi_1(t)=1\) with \(\phi_2(t)=1+t\) on \([-1,0]\). Obtain the distinct states on the first interval.

Work through the solution

For the first history \(y_1=1-t\). For the second, \(\phi_2(t-1)=t\), so \(y_2'= -t\) and \(y_2=1-t^2/2\). At \(t=1\) their values are 0 and 0.5 although both began at 1. Their initial right derivatives differ by one. A restart record containing only time and last state cannot reconstruct the interrupted integration; retaining the relevant past function is part of the state representation.

Compare physical and discrete stability boundaries

A controller has \(a=4\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). Estimate the first critical delay and the oscillation period there. What numerical checks would separate it from a coarse-step instability?

Work through the solution

The first critical delay is

\[\pi/(2a)=0.3927\,\mathrm{s}.\]

Its frequency is \(a/(2\pi)=0.6366\) Hz and period \(2\pi/a=1.571\) s. Change the delay on both sides of the predicted value, resolve the oscillation with multiple step sizes and compare measured growth with the characteristic equation. Keep history interpolation accurate while refining; fixed low-order delayed lookup can determine the observed stability error even as the main integrator step is reduced.
