Dynamics and numerical time integration · 3. Solving a two-point boundary problem without guessing the start slope

When endpoint conditions act on different sides of an interval, shooting can amplify a tiny initial error. Construct a cubic collocation system, test its continuous differential residual and quantify which boundary-value solution the residual actually certifies.

Sensitivity of shooting need not be sensitivity of the physical solution

Take \(y''=\kappa^2y\) on \(0\le x\le1\), with \(y(0)=1,\ y(1)=0\). The solution \(y=\sinh[\kappa(1-x)]/\sinh\kappa\) decays from the left. To shoot from the left, introduce unknown slope \(s\), yielding endpoint \(y(1)=\cosh\kappa+(s/\kappa)\sinh\kappa\). The correct slope is \(-\kappa\coth\kappa\) and its error is magnified by \(\sinh\kappa/\kappa\) at the far end. For \(\kappa=20\) this factor is about \(1.21\times10^7\). Even an initial slope accurate to \(10^{-10}\) can cause a far-end mismatch of order \(10^{-3}\). The mathematical boundary solution can remain well behaved while this particular representation suffers cancellation of large growing and decaying components.

A collocation boundary solver instead treats approximate states at many locations as simultaneous unknowns and enforces both boundary conditions directly. Write the equation as first-order system \(Y'=(v,\kappa^2 y)^T=F(x,Y)\), \(Y=(y,v)\). On each panel \([x_i,x_{i+1}]\) choose a cubic Hermite polynomial using unknown endpoint states and endpoint slopes fixed by \(F_i,F_{i+1}\). This already imposes the differential equation at both endpoints. Require the polynomial derivative to satisfy the ODE also at the midpoint. Matching yields two equations for each panel, with the boundary equations closing the overall algebraic system.

Derive a residual with only three right-side evaluations per panel

For panel width \(h\), Hermite interpolation at the midpoint gives

\[Y_m=(Y_i+Y_{i+1})/2+h(F_i-F_{i+1})/8.\]

Its derivative there is \(p'_m=3(Y_{i+1}-Y_i)/(2h)-(F_i+F_{i+1})/4\). Equating this with \(F_m=F(x_i+h/2,Y_m)\) yields

\[Y_{i+1}-Y_i-\frac h6(F_i+4F_m+F_{i+1})=0 .\]

This is a three-point Lobatto collocation relation, resembling Simpson quadrature with a midpoint state determined by the same cubic. Neighbouring panels share endpoint states and slopes, constructing a continuously differentiable piecewise approximation for a smooth right side. The nonlinear algebraic Jacobian has local block structure apart from boundary conditions and a few global unknowns. Dense finite differencing of the entire state would discard that structure and can dominate a large adaptive solve.

As a simple independent test take the scalar ODE \(y'=\lambda y\). With \(z=h\lambda\), substitution of \(Y_m\) yields a one-panel transfer \(R(z)=(1+z/2+z^2/12)/(1-z/2+z^2/12)\), agreeing with \(e^z\) through \(z^4\). The symmetry \(R(-z)=1/R(z)\) checks the derivation. At large negative \(z\), \(R(z)\to1\), so the local solution formula is not strongly damping for unresolved stiff modes. Global enforcement of boundary conditions and adaptive spatial resolution remain important: a stable algebraic solve need not resolve a very thin physical layer.

Interpret a continuous residual through a stability estimate

A collocation residual vanishes at the points where it was imposed, so checking only those values tests the nonlinear algebraic solve, not the differential approximation between points. Evaluate the continuous defect \(d(x)=p'(x)-F(x,p(x))\) at independent points or use its suitably weighted panel norm. Refine where the residual is large, especially where the solution changes rapidly, and enforce the boundary conditions independently. A mesh with many uniform far-interior points can inefficiently resolve a solution having a layer of thickness \(1/\kappa\) near a single wall.

For a scalar linear elliptic boundary problem \(-y''+\kappa^2 y=f\) with zero endpoint error, an approximation with smooth enough derivatives has error governed by \(-e''+\kappa^2e=r\). If \(\|r\|_\infty\le\epsilon\), a maximum-principle argument gives \(\|e\|_\infty\le\epsilon/\kappa^2\) for \(\kappa>0\): at an interior positive error maximum, \(-e''\ge0\), and similarly for the negative minimum. This bound links a differential residual in the same formulation to solution error under the stated boundary conditions. A residual in a differently scaled first-order system requires its own inverse stability estimate. Near a nonunique boundary solution an apparently tiny defect can correspond to a much larger state error.

Reconstruct the entire cubic boundary solution

The calculator solves the same linear collocation equations as the written panel derivation. For this constant matrix, its growing and shrinking eigendirections decouple and one panel applies the stated rational transfer to each. The numerical field shown between nodes is the actual cubic Hermite interpolant through those collocation states and their slopes. The reference is the independently evaluated hyperbolic-sine boundary solution. Increase the layer parameter at fixed panel count first and look just inside the left boundary. Both curves hit the imposed endpoints even on a visibly inadequate mesh.

Then double the number of panels. The displayed maximum difference is measured on 241 independent evaluation locations and is a sampled diagnostic, not a guaranteed global norm. For large layer parameter the shooting endpoint amplification can be enormous even while the collocation solution becomes accurate upon mesh refinement. At only a few cells across the layer the cubic may bend below zero. A solution satisfying the nonlinear algebraic conditions of the chosen discretisation need not preserve positivity of the continuum field at every point.

Open the live experiment

Tutorial · distinguish mesh, boundary and algebraic errors

Check a cubic against an exactly representable solution

Choose \(y'=3x^2,\ y(0)=0\) on a single unit panel. Verify the collocation residual using the candidate \(y=x^3\) and identify the midpoint state from endpoint data.

Work through the solution

Endpoint values are 0 and 1, slopes 0 and 3. The Hermite midpoint value is \(1/2-(3/8)=1/8\), equal to \((1/2)^3\). The midpoint slope is \(3/4\). Simpson's right side gives \([0+4(3/4)+3]/6=1\), equal to the endpoint difference. Here the entire state polynomial, not just its grid values, is exact. This manufactured case checks signs and factors before using the solver on a stiff boundary layer.

Quantify endpoint sensitivity for one shooting trial

At \(\kappa=10\), an independent integration has a starting-slope error \(2\times10^{-7}\). Estimate its endpoint error. Would measuring that endpoint residual accurately imply the underlying boundary problem is ill conditioned?

Work through the solution

The amplification is \(\sinh10/10\simeq1101.3\), yielding endpoint error \(2.20\times10^{-4}\). This exposes sensitivity of a one-way initial-value representation to the guessed slope. A boundary perturbation instead propagates through bounded functions \(\sinh[\kappa(1-x)]/\sinh\kappa\) and \(\sinh(\kappa x)/\sinh\kappa\). Its effect on interior \(y\) need not contain the huge shooting factor. Distinguish sensitivity of the original boundary data from cancellation in one numerical strategy.

Spot a false residual certificate

A computation checks only \(p'-F=0\) at each panel's two ends and midpoint and reports error below \(10^{-12}\). What evidence is still needed for a claimed \(10^{-6}\) physical solution tolerance?

Work through the solution

Those are exactly the collocation equations; their tiny residual tests the solved discrete system. Evaluate the defect off those nodes or estimate its norm by an independent quadrature, verify boundary mismatches, and relate these defects to solution error using a suitable stability bound or independent reference refinement. For the second-order model with \(\kappa=5\) and zero endpoint error a uniform residual below \(25\times10^{-6}\) in \(-p''+25p-f\) suffices for the stated magnitude bound. A reported first-order-system residual with different units cannot be inserted unchanged.

Further reading

  • Kierzenka and Shampine · a boundary-value solver controlling residual and error
  • MATLAB bvp4c · three-stage Lobatto collocation
  • Course investigation · sensitivity of boundary-value shooting
  • Compare time-step error with conservation

Dynamics and numerical time integration · 4. Why stiff stability does not guarantee the expected order

A differential equation is constructed to have a smooth, known solution and a rapidly damped error mode. Use it to distinguish stability of the homogeneous equation, tracking of a moving target and the effect of off-grid stage forcing.

Choose an exact solution before increasing stiffness

Let \(\lambda<0\), let \(\phi(t)\) be a smooth specified function, and solve the Prothero–Robinson model \(y'=\lambda[y-\phi(t)]+\phi'(t)\), \(y(0)=\phi(0)\). The exact solution is \(y(t)=\phi(t)\) for every \(\lambda\). For an initial mismatch \(e_0\), the error evolves \(e(t)=e_0e^{\lambda t}\). Thus the observed slow solution and fast relaxation timescale can be varied independently. This makes the model an incisive verification case for a stiff integrator: changing \(\lambda\) leaves the exact waveform fixed while altering how errors created by discrete stages are damped.

A stability function \(R(h\lambda)\) describes propagation of errors for the homogeneous test \(y'=\lambda y\). A-stability constrains \(|R(z)|\le1\) over the left half-plane. L-stability additionally requires \(R(z)\to0\) as \(|z|\to\infty\) in the stable stiff limit. Neither definition alone describes how a method evaluates a time-dependent forcing at its internal stages. Stage order and stiff accuracy can matter when those stage errors are multiplied by large \(|\lambda|\). Observing bounded output on a homogeneous damping test does not establish a method's full formal order on a forced stiff problem.

Calculate the error recurrence of backward Euler and the trapezoidal rule

Write \(e_n=y_n-\phi_n,\ z=h\lambda\). Backward Euler evaluates the whole right side at \(t_{n+1}\). Collect terms after subtracting the exact target:

\[(1-z)e_{n+1}=e_n+\phi_n-\phi_{n+1}+h\phi'_{n+1}.\]

The forcing defect begins at \(h^2\phi''_{n+1}/2\). Its effect on the next error is divided by \(1-z\). The rapidly decaying homogeneous mode is also divided by that factor. This can make backward Euler track a particular smooth imposed target surprisingly well in the very stiff regime even though its classical nonstiff order is one. Its behaviour should be assessed in the desired observables and step–stiffness range, rather than ranked solely by the formal method label.

For the trapezoidal rule the corresponding equation is

\[(1-z/2)e_{n+1}=(1+z/2)e_n+\phi_n-\phi_{n+1}+\tfrac h2(\phi_n'+\phi_{n+1}').\]

The defect is the trapezoidal quadrature error in the derivative and begins at order \(h^3\). However, its homogeneous factor tends to \(-1\) when \(z\to-\infty\), so a pre-existing fast error can survive as a sign-alternating numerical component for many steps. With \(z=-100\) the factors are \(1/101=0.00990\) for backward Euler and \(-49/51=-0.96078\) for trapezoid. Both schemes are A-stable. Only the first immediately damps this unresolved mode. Event resets, inconsistent initial preparation or changing external loads can seed it.

A midpoint constraint can force endpoint drift

Implicit midpoint uses \(y_{n+1}-y_n=h[\lambda((y_n+y_{n+1})/2-\phi_m)+\phi'_m]\). For fixed \(h\) and large negative \(\lambda\), finite numerical states require the stage average to approach \(\phi_m\). Thus \(y_{n+1}\simeq2\phi_m-y_n\). The endpoints inferred from this interior constraint need not land on \(\phi_{n+1}\). Compare with trapezoid, which shares exactly the same scalar homogeneous stability function but evaluates two different times and states for nonlinear or time-dependent forcing.

With \(y_n=\phi_n\) and \(\phi(t)=t^2\), the limiting endpoint error is

\[2\phi(t_n+h/2)-\phi_n-\phi_{n+1}=-h^2/2.\]

The exact finite-\(z\) midpoint error starting from the target is \(zh^2/[4(1-z/2)]\). The trapezoidal derivative quadrature, in contrast, is exact for this quadratic target and keeps \(e_n=0\) at every step started exactly on it. Their common stability function did not predict their different forced response. In the midpoint example an alternating error can cancel at some output times, so inspecting only the final point at an even number of steps may obscure the largest per-step error. Compare all sampled times and separate propagated from newly injected defects.

Separate injected forcing error from a starting mismatch

This experiment uses the target t squared with step 0.1 s. It propagates the exact error recurrences of backward Euler, trapezoid and midpoint rather than estimating the difference by subtracting two large trajectories. The reference also includes the exponentially decaying initial mismatch. Move the stiffness control until a time step contains a hundred fast relaxation times. With a nonzero initial error, trapezoid and midpoint preserve a visible alternating component even though the exact mismatch essentially disappears after one step.

Set the initial mismatch to zero to isolate what an initially accurate trajectory gains from stage forcing. For this one quadratic target the trapezoidal derivative quadrature is exact and introduces no error, while midpoint still misses the endpoint because it constrains the value at the middle of the interval. Try a less stiff value and compare the peak and endpoint errors over all times. The demonstration is deliberately a manufactured exact target; substituting a cubic target would remove trapezoid’s special cancellation while keeping the same homogeneous stability function.

Open the live experiment

Tutorial · compare error mechanisms at fixed work

Compute a forced stiff step exactly

Set \(h=0.10,\lambda=-1000,\phi=t^2,\ y_0=0\). Compute errors after the first step for backward Euler, trapezoid and midpoint.

Work through the solution

Here \(z=-100\), \(\phi(h)=0.01\). Backward Euler defect is \(h^2\), giving \(e_1=0.01/101=9.90\times10^{-5}\). Trapezoid yields zero error on this target. Midpoint gives \(z h^2/[4(1-z/2)]=-0.004902\), so its predicted \(y_1\) is only 0.005098. The exact target is smooth in all three experiments; the fast stiffness multiplies the way its curvature is sampled within the step.

Check an unresolved initial-layer perturbation

Keep \(z=-100\) and introduce an initial error of 0.01. How large is the propagated part after two steps, excluding new forcing defects?

Work through the solution

The exact propagated part is \(0.01e^{-200}\), negligible here. Backward Euler gives

\[0.01/101^2=9.80\times10^{-7}.\]

Both trapezoid and implicit midpoint give \(0.01(49/51)^2=0.00923\) with restored positive sign after the second step. Negative sign at alternate steps can be invisible if the code saves every second state. Monitoring stage and consecutive-step output helps identify this persistence.

Design a refinement that distinguishes ordinary order from stiff forcing error

Why are a study at fixed \(\lambda\) with \(h\to0\) and one at fixed \(h\lambda=-100\) different experiments? What should be plotted?

Work through the solution

At fixed \(\lambda\), sufficiently small \(h\) eventually resolves the fast timescale and standard smooth-solution order can reappear. Holding \(h\lambda=-100\) changes the differential equation's error-relaxation scale at every refinement, deliberately keeping that fast mode unresolved while the exact target stays fixed. Plot maximum error over all physical sample times, the homogeneous amplification and representative stage defects for both sequences. Report the exact initial mismatch as well as \(\phi\). Otherwise a measured order can conflate target tracking, unremoved fast transients and cancellation at one favourable output time.

  • Prothero and Robinson · stability and accuracy of one-step methods for stiff systems

Dynamics and numerical time integration · 5. How far does an energy-preserving rotation lag behind?

Integrate uniform circular motion by the implicit midpoint rule. The points stay exactly on the energy circle in exact arithmetic. Derive the angle advanced at each step and measure the accumulated timing error.

From a midpoint balance to a rotation matrix

Write a two-component oscillator as \(v'=\omega Jv\), where \(J\) sends \((x,y)\) to \((-y,x)\). Choosing scaled coordinates makes both components comparable; for a mechanical oscillator one may take displacement and a suitably scaled speed. The generator is skew-symmetric, so \(d(v^Tv)/dt=\omega v^T(J^T+J)v=0\). The exact motion has angular advance \(h\omega\) over an interval \(h\).

Midpoint approximates the velocity at the midpoint of the segment joining the old and new states. This yields \((I-aJ)v_{n+1}=(I+aJ)v_n\) with \(a=h\omega/2\). Since \(J^2=-I\), multiplication by \(I+aJ\) eliminates the inverse and gives

\[R_h=(I-aJ)^{-1}(I+aJ)=\frac{(1-a^2)I+2aJ}{1+a^2}.\]

The coefficients of \(I\) and \(J\) have squares summing to one. They are the cosine and sine of the angle \(\theta_h=2\arctan a\). Thus every update preserves the squared radius. The angle differs from the exact \(h\omega\). Expanding the arctangent gives

\[\theta_h=h\omega-(h\omega)^3/12+O((h\omega)^5).\]

Over a fixed duration \(T=nh\), the leading accumulated angle error is \(-T\omega^3h^2/12\). This phase lag is especially visible after many periods even when a graph of energy looks perfect.

For comparison, explicit Euler takes the tangent line to its end point. Its amplification is \(\sqrt{1+(h\omega)^2}\). Studying both amplitude and timing identifies different defects of the two updates.

Put a clock on the unit circle

Let \(\omega=2\) rad/s, \(h=0.5\) s and \(v_0=(1,0)\). The half-step parameter is 0.5. The rotation sends the first point to \((0.6,0.8)\) instead of the exact \((\cos1,\sin1)\approx(0.5403,0.8415)\). Both radii are one. The angular deficit is \(1-2\arctan(0.5)\approx0.072705\) rad per update. After ten updates, the numerical clock is behind by 0.72705 rad or about 0.364 s of physical time.

With \(h=0.25\) s over the same five seconds the deficit is \(10-40\arctan(0.25)\approx0.20085\) rad. Its ratio to the coarse-step error is close to one quarter. In the notebook the rotation is built by a linear solve and its angle is measured from a dot product and an oriented area. These are independent ways to check the hand formula. Counting full turns requires retaining unwrapped increments; a final point alone cannot identify the number of completed turns.

Exercise · reverse the sign of time

Show why a forward midpoint update followed by one equal backward update returns to the starting state. Does forward Euler have the same property here?

Work through the solution

Replacing \(a\) by \(-a\) interchanges the two factors in the Cayley formula, so \(R_{-h}=R_h^{-1}\). Their product is the identity. For explicit Euler the product is \((I-h\omega J)(I+h\omega J)=(1+(h\omega)^2)I\). The reversal increases every radius when the angular speed and the step are nonzero.

Exercise · set a phase budget

For \(\omega=2\) rad/s, approximate a time step that keeps the accumulated phase lag below 0.02 rad over six seconds. Give an exact way to check the choice.

Work through the solution

The leading magnitude is \(T\omega^3h^2/12=4h^2\). Equating this to 0.02 gives about 0.0707 s. Choosing 90 equal steps over six seconds gives \(h=1/15\) s. The exact lag is \(12-180\arctan(1/15)\) rad, about 0.0177. The exact check is useful when the step is large enough that the cubic series term has limited accuracy.

Dynamics and numerical time integration · 6. From existence of a trajectory to the order of a time step

Establish what an initial-value solver approximates, derive Runge–Kutta order conditions for a general smooth vector field and construct an alternative high-order step by extrapolation.

Construct the trajectory on a short interval

Let \(f(t,y)\) be continuous on a time–state cylinder, bounded there by \(M\) and Lipschitz in \(y\) with constant \(L\). For initial value \(y_0\), seek a continuous curve in the closed sup-norm ball of radius \(R\) around \(y_0\). On an interval of length \(H\), apply

\[(Tz)(t)=y_0+\int_{t_0}^{t} f(s,z(s))\,ds.\]

If \(HM\le R\) and \(HL<1\), \(T\) maps the ball into itself and is a contraction. Iteration from the constant curve changes by at most \(HL\) times the preceding change. The geometric series of increments converges uniformly and the limit satisfies the integral equation. Differentiation gives the ODE. Two such limits have distance at most \(HL\) times their distance, hence coincide. Overlapping short cylinders extend the trajectory until continuation fails. For \(y'=y^2,\ y(0)=1\), the explicit \(1/(1-t)\) illustrates why local existence gives no promise at \(t=1\).

The perturbed integral inequality \(e(t)\le e_0+\int_0^t(Le(s)+d(s))ds\) leads, by an integrating factor for the right-hand side, to \(e(t)\le e^{Lt}e_0+\int_0^t e^{L(t-s)}d(s)ds\). It explains why a finite-time global error bound for a numerical solver needs an appropriate domain and a perturbation-growth estimate.

Collect elementary derivatives without losing branching terms

For an autonomous smooth \(f\), the first three elementary differentials are \(f,\ f'f,\ f''(f,f)\), together with the third-order chain \(f'(f'f)\). Represent a derivative acting on child differentials by a node with one branch per argument. A single node has order and density one. Recursively, a tree \(\tau=[\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_k]\) has \(|\tau|=1+\sum|\tau_j|\) and density \(\gamma(\tau)=|\tau|\prod\gamma(\tau_j)\). This factor follows by integrating the power of time once when a root is added. Multiplicities of identical branches give the same symmetry factor on the exact and numerical sides.

Define stage weights \(\Phi_i(\bullet)=1\). Taylor expanding each RK stage about the initial state replaces an integral for every child by a weighted stage sum. Consequently

\[\Phi_i([\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_k])=\prod_{\ell=1}^k\sum_j a_{ij}\Phi_j(\tau_\ell),\]
\[\sum_i b_i\Phi_i(\tau)=1/\gamma(\tau).\]

Matching every tree through order \(p\) gives classical order \(p\) for general sufficiently smooth vector fields. The local defect is order \(p+1\), yielding global order \(p\) under the perturbation bound above. For the fourth-order tree consisting of a root, a child, and two leaves on that child, the condition is \(b^TAc^2=1/12\), with \(c=A\mathbf1\) and the square componentwise. For a root with three leaves it is \(b^Tc^3=1/4\). A scalar linear test equation supplies only chains.

Select accuracy by spending more substeps

For a smooth problem over one macrostep \(H\), let \(T_{j,0}\) be its endpoint from \(N_j=2^j\) Euler substeps. At fixed \(H\), assume the endpoint error has an asymptotic expansion in powers of \(1/N_j\). This follows for smooth solutions by expanding the local error and propagating its coefficients. The leading term halves on doubling. Eliminate successive powers by

\[T_{j,k}=T_{j,k-1}+\frac{T_{j,k-1}-T_{j-1,k-1}}{2^k-1}.\]

For \(y'=y,\ y(0)=1,\ H=0.2\), the one- and two-substep estimates are \(1.2\) and \(1.21\), so the extrapolate is \(1.22\), close to \(e^{0.2}\). Four substeps give \(1.21550625\). Extrapolating once gives \(1.2210125\) and twice gives \(1.22135\). Use the separation between successive columns to estimate the lower-order local error. For a column of global order \(k+1\), a local tolerance suggests a trial next \(H\) proportional to the tolerance ratio raised to \(1/(k+2)\) if using that column's error estimate. Compare accepted advance per function evaluation at candidate orders. Restart the expansion after a discontinuity and impose the stability restriction of the underlying method.

When can a correct extrapolation tableau be misleading?

An unresolved event or a nonsmooth forcing invalidates the common power expansion across grids. Agreement among columns can then mean that every grid missed the same feature. Split at a known event and test the reconstruction against the defect in the integral equation.

Tutorial on existence and order

Bound the Picard iteration for a linear cooling model

Work through the solution

With \(f=-2y+1,\ y_0=1,\ R=1\), \(|f|\le3\) and \(L=2\) on \([0,2]\). Taking \(H=1/4\) gives \(HM=3/4\) and contraction ratio at most \(1/2\). The sum of all remaining differences after an iterate is bounded by the last difference times \((1/2)/(1-1/2)\).

Distinguish two fourth-order calculations experimentally

Work through the solution

A linear exponential tests the chain \(b^TA^2c\) and is blind to \(f''\). To excite branching terms choose a smooth nonlinear vector field and compare to an independent fine solution at successively halved step sizes. A nonlinear test can reveal an incorrect coefficient, but the general order proof needs every independent tree condition.

Dynamics and numerical time integration · 7. Why consistent multistep formulas can fail under refinement

Derive the root condition for constant steps, propagate a local defect to a fixed end time and isolate a growing parasitic mode with a controlled starting error.

Turn the recurrence into a propagation matrix

Write a \(k\)-step scheme as \(\sum_{j=0}^k\alpha_j y_{n+j}=h\sum_{j=0}^k\beta_jf_{n+j}\) with \(\alpha_k=1\). Let \(\rho(z)=\sum\alpha_jz^j\) and \(\sigma(z)=\sum\beta_jz^j\). Substituting exact constant and linear solutions shows consistency requires \(\rho(1)=0,\ \rho'(1)=\sigma(1)\). For continuous differentiable exact solutions these conditions make the defect divided by \(h\) tend to zero. Higher derivatives determine the local order.

For \(f=0\) stack the latest \(k\) errors. Their propagation matrix is the companion matrix \(C\) of \(\rho\). A root \(r\) of multiplicity \(s\) contributes modes \(n^\ell r^n,\ 0\le\ell<s\) for nonzero roots. All powers of \(C\) stay bounded exactly when all characteristic roots lie in the closed unit disk and unit-modulus roots are simple. Interior repeated roots are harmless because exponential decay dominates their polynomial factors. This is null stability for constant step sequences.

Sum the defects over a fixed time

Let a convergent starter have maximum error \(E_0\), the local discrete defect be \(h\,\tau_n\), and \(f\) be Lipschitz with constant \(L\) on the solutions' domain. Repeated substitution using the bounded \(C^j\) first propagates the starter and each past perturbation. The Lipschitz terms contribute a sum of errors. For an implicit step take \(h\) sufficiently small to absorb its newest error term. With constants independent of \(h\),

\[E_n\le C_1(E_0+T\max|\tau_j|)+C_2h\sum_{i<n}E_i \le C_1(E_0+T\max|\tau_j|)\exp(C_2T).\]

Thus consistency and null stability imply convergence. Conversely, convergence for arbitrary convergent starting values on smooth problems forces bounded homogeneous propagation. If a root lies outside the disk, a vanishing initial perturbation can grow exponentially over \(T/h\) steps. For a repeated unit root choose an initial perturbation proportional to \(h^{s-1}\) in the generalised mode so that its final error stays finite and nonzero. Consistency is forced by testing constants and linear-in-time solutions under convergence for all equations, taking an irreducible multistep formula and the usual normalisation. This equivalence concerns fixed-order, constant-step schemes. Changing step ratios changes the propagation matrices.

Control the perturbation before changing the grid

For \(f=0\), take \(\rho=(z-1)(z-r)\), with \(r=-1\) or \(r=2\). Choose \(\sigma=1-r\) to make the associated formulas consistent. Initialise \(y_0=1,y_1=1+h^3\). The exact propagated endpoint error at \(N=1/h\) is

\[e_N=h^3(1-r^N)/(1-r).\]

For \(r=-1\) every error stays \(O(h^3)\). For \(r=2\) the growing factor eventually defeats every algebraic power. Exact starting data with no perturbations would hide this instability in the constant solution.

Transfer the experiment to the decaying state \(y'=-y\) by choosing \(\sigma(z)=(1-r)z\). Now the middle coefficient in the recurrence is \(1+r-h(1-r)\). A OneSimulation RK4 trajectory supplies a common starter and a separate full-step trajectory checked against \(e^{-1}\). Inject \(h^3\) into the starter and compare with the identical multistep solve without that injection. The difference isolates amplification along the recurrence.

OneSimulation experiment · Watch a vanishing starting error grow

Vary the number of steps and the injected starting error. Compare both perturbation amplification and the error of the reference trajectory.

Download the executable notebook · Python source

Adjust the experiment in the browser. Vary the parasitic root, the step count and the injected starting error independently. The first step is the RK4 polynomial for \(y'=-y\); subsequent steps use the two-step recurrence above. The browser follows the perturbation in its own recurrence so that subtractive cancellation does not hide a tiny change. Compare \(r=-1,0,1,2\). At \(r=1\), the repeated root and vanishing differential term make this a degenerate, unstable formula. Hold the perturbation fixed to inspect amplification; then lower it by a factor of eight when doubling the number of steps to reproduce an \(h^3\) perturbation. The notebook uses OneSimulation RK4 for its starter and reference trajectory.

Open the live experiment

"""Resolve a decaying signal with two consistent families of two-step methods."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.time import ClassicalRungeKutta4TimeIntegrator

def decay(state, time):
    return (-state[0],)

def endpoint(parasitic, starter, h, n):
    previous, current = 1.0, starter
    for _ in range(1, n):
        previous, current = current, (1+parasitic-h*(1-parasitic))*current-parasitic*previous
    return current

method = ClassicalRungeKutta4TimeIntegrator()
counts = [6, 10, 14, 18, 22, 26]
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 4), layout="constrained")
for parasitic in [-1, 2]:
    errors = []
    for n in counts:
        h = 1/n
        reference = (1.0,)
        starter = method.advance(reference, decay, h, time=0.)[0]
        for j in range(n):
            reference = method.advance(reference, decay, h, time=j*h)
        unperturbed = endpoint(parasitic, starter, h, n)
        perturbed = endpoint(parasitic, starter+h**3, h, n)
        errors.append(abs(perturbed-unperturbed))
        assert abs(reference[0] - np.exp(-1)) < 1e-5
    ax.semilogy(counts, errors, "o-", label=f"Parasitic root {parasitic:+d}")
    print(f"Root {parasitic:+d}: change at T=1 with 26 steps: {errors[-1]:.6g}")
print(f"Reference RK4 error with 26 steps: {abs(reference[0]-np.exp(-1)):.3g}")
ax.set(xlabel="Steps to time 1", ylabel="Final change caused by starting perturbation")
ax.legend()
fig.savefig("numerics-parasitic-root.png", dpi=170)

Output from the reference run

Root -1: change at T=1 with 26 steps: 6.6793e-05
Root +2: change at T=1 with 26 steps: 8875.58
Reference RK4 error with 26 steps: 6.93e-09

Tutorial on multiplicity and error order

Can two roots at one be tolerated if every local defect is tiny?

Work through the solution

For \(\rho=(z-1)^2\) and \(f=0\), choose \(e_0=0,e_1=h\). Differences stay equal to \(h\), so \(e_N=Nh=T\). The local residual is exactly zero while the starter converges and the endpoint fails to converge.

Separate the order of the starter from that of the method

Work through the solution

A null-stable order-three formula has \(\tau=O(h^3)\). If its starter has only \(O(h^2)\) accuracy, the bound at fixed \(T\) becomes \(O(h^2)\). Raising the order of the multistep formula alone cannot recover the lost order.

Hairer, Nørsett and Wanner · multistep methods

Dynamics and numerical time integration · 8. Why an accurate position can hide an inaccurate constraint force

Derive an energy test for implicit Runge–Kutta stages, then recover missing derivatives in a constrained model and calculate the accuracy lost by a multiplier.

Derive a nonlinear contractivity test

Suppose \(\langle u-v,f(u)-f(v)\rangle\le0\) for all relevant states. For two trajectories advanced by the same RK step, use \(D_i\) for each stage-state difference and \(g_i\) for the stage-slope differences. Substitute the stage identity \(D_i=d_0+h\sum_j a_{ij}g_j\) into the square of \(d_1=d_0+h\sum_i b_i g_i\). Collecting symmetric products gives

\[\|d_1\|^2-\|d_0\|^2=2h\sum_i b_i\langle D_i,g_i\rangle-h^2\sum_{ij}(b_i a_{ij}+b_j a_{ji}-b_i b_j)\langle g_i,g_j\rangle .\]

If \(b_i\ge0\) and the displayed symmetric coefficient matrix is positive semidefinite, the step cannot increase distances, assuming its nonlinear stage equations have a solution. This sufficient algebraic stability condition applies to nonlinear dissipative fields. For one-stage midpoint \(a=1/2,b=1\) the matrix is zero. Backward Euler \(a=b=1\) gives one. Both are contractive, but their scalar amplification limits as \(h\lambda\to-\infty\) are \(-1\) and 0 respectively. Only the latter damps an unresolved fast mode completely.

Recover the derivatives needed to see the multiplier

For index one, \(y'=f(y,z),\ 0=g(y,z)\) with \(g_z\) invertible, the implicit function theorem eliminates \(z\). Perturbing the algebraic constraint at fixed \(y\) by \(\epsilon\) gives \(\delta z=O(\epsilon/\sigma_{\min}(g_z))\). A mechanical position constraint has a different structure:

\[q'=v,\]
\[Mv'=F(q,v)-G(q)^T\lambda,\]
\[g(q)=0,\]
\[G=g_q .\]

Differentiating once gives \(Gv=0\). Differentiating again gives \(Gv'+g_{qq}[v,v]=0\). For positive definite mass and independent constraints the multiplier matrix \(GM^{-1}G^T\) is invertible and

\[(GM^{-1}G^T)\lambda=GM^{-1}F+g_{qq}[v,v].\]

The position formulation is a differentiation-index-three system in first-order form. Consistent initial data require both position and velocity constraints. A small position residual alone places no bound on multiplier error without regularity of its time dependence.

Derive a concrete order loss before interpreting an index

A manufactured constraint \(q=\sin t\) with \(q'=v,\ v'=\lambda\) has \(v=\cos t,\lambda=-\sin t\). Suppose a position solver introduces an alternating error \(e_n=(-1)^nh^p\). A backward difference for velocity amplifies it to \(2(-1)^n h^{p-1}\), and a second difference for the multiplier amplifies it to \(4(-1)^n h^{p-2}\). A smooth error series instead can gain cancellations. This distinction prevents assigning every index-three method a universal loss of two orders. Consistent stage order, constraint enforcement, regularity, initial conditions and the reconstruction used for the multiplier all affect the theorem.

Consider a converged Radau collocation step for the simpler index-two constraint \(q' =z,\ q=g(t)\). Its stages enforce the exact sampled \(g(t_n+c_i h)\). Starting from exact \(q_n\), the derivative stages equal those of the degree-\(s\) interpolant through the start and the \(s\) stage nodes. Stiff accuracy identifies the final stage as the output. Interpolant differentiation gives endpoint derivative error \(O(h^s)\) for smooth \(g\). The constrained position is exact here, illustrating precisely how classical differential-variable order fails to predict a multiplier measurement.

Derive an index-dependent rate in the constant-coefficient case

For a regular linear differential-algebraic pencil with constant coefficients, the algebraic part can be written as \(Nz'=z-g(t)\), where \(N^\nu=0\) and \(N^{\nu-1}\ne0\). Repeated substitution gives \(z=g+Ng'+\cdots+N^{\nu-1}g^{(\nu-1)}\). For a single Jordan chain, its last component specifies the input directly while the first can depend on its derivative of order \(\nu-1\). The highest derivative actually seen depends on the input direction and the reported component.

Interpolate \(g\) through \(s+1\) distinct nodes at fixed relative positions on an interval of length \(h\). Taylor expansion at the endpoint through degree \(s\) is exact for the interpolant. The residual nodal values are \(O(h^{s+1})\). Differentiating the fixed reference cardinal polynomials \(\ell\) times introduces \(h^{-\ell}\), giving \(O(h^{s+1-\ell})\) for derivative order \(\ell\le s\). Smoothness through degree \(s+1\) is required. If nodal observations themselves carry \(O(h^p)\) disturbances, their differentiated contribution is \(O(h^{p-\ell})\). The worst reconstructed component on an index-\(\nu\) chain therefore has order \(\min(s+1,p)-(\nu-1)\), when \(\nu-1\le s\) and the forcing couples to that last derivative. This is an error bound for this specified reconstruction. A different integrator's stage order and enforcement of the constraints need a separate analysis.

What does a nonlinear stage tolerance control?

A stage solve tolerance on position has the units of position. When converting residuals to a velocity or acceleration tolerance, budget the factors \(1/h\) and \(1/h^2\) and the conditioning of the constraint matrix. Plot force error on a manufactured trajectory as well as the position-constraint residual.

Tutorial on contractivity and constraint data

Check the algebraic stability test on forward Euler

Work through the solution

With \(a=0,b=1\), the matrix coefficient is \(-1\). For \(y'=-y\), the amplification \(1-h\) has magnitude above one for \(h>2\), confirming that increasing the step may violate contractivity.

What force holds a unit mass on the unit circle at speed three?

Work through the solution

Choose \(g=(q^Tq-1)/2\), so \(G=q^T\), \(g_{qq}=I\), and \(F=0\). With \(\|q\|=1,\ q^Tv=0\), the multiplier is \(\|v\|^2=9\) and the constraint force is \(-9q\). An outward initial velocity violates the hidden constraint even if the position is exact.

Hairer and Wanner · stiff and differential-algebraic equations

Conserving motion in a physical system

Track an invariant in phase space. Compare a rotating charge, parametrically driven oscillator and magnetic orbit. Use phase and energy as independent error measurements.

  1. Hamiltonian motion in phase space
  2. Nonlinear stiffness and amplitude-dependent period
  3. Liouville flow and what an ensemble can remember
  4. Parametric resonance and Floquet growth
  5. A magnetic orbit that spirals outward in a perfect vacuum
  6. Relativistic particle dynamics and the field tensor
  7. Euler–Poinsot motion, torque-free nutation and the intermediate axis
  8. Symplectic transformations, Poisson brackets and the geometry of measurements
  9. Canonical perturbations, small denominators and resonant capture
  10. Distinguish a physical precession from numerical drift

Hamiltonian motion in phase space

Classical mechanics

Construct a Hamiltonian, read a phase portrait and distinguish bounded motion from rotation at a separatrix.

From first principles

Construct Hamilton’s equations by exchanging velocity for momentum

Begin with a smooth Lagrangian \(L(q,\dot q,t)\) for a conservative mechanical system. Assume the momentum definition \(p=\partial_{\dot q}L\) can locally be inverted to give \(\dot q\) as a function of \((q,p,t)\). This regularity is what lets position and momentum serve as independent state coordinates. Define the Hamiltonian and differentiate the product before substituting any equations of motion:

\[H=p\dot q-L,\]
\[dH=\dot q\,dp+p\,d\dot q-L_q\,dq-L_{\dot q}\,d\dot q-L_t\,dt .\]

The two \(d\dot q\) terms cancel by the momentum definition. Comparing the coefficients of the independent differentials gives

\[H_p=\dot q,\]
\[H_q=-L_q.\]

Hamilton's principle previously gave the Lagrange equation \(\dot p=L_q\), so

\[\dot q=\partial_pH,\]
\[\dot p=-\partial_qH,\]
\[\partial_tH=-\partial_tL .\]

Along a solution the \(q\) and \(p\) contributions to \(dH/dt\) cancel. For a time-independent Hamiltonian \(H\) is constant, so planar trajectories lie on its level sets. In the elementary case \(L=m\dot q^2/2-U(q)\), the transformation yields

\[H=p^2/(2m)+U(q).\]
Background · Generalised momentum
\[p_i=\partial L/\partial\dot q_i\]

Its units depend on the coordinate. Conjugacy to an angle gives an angular momentum. Conservation requires the associated coordinate to be cyclic in the chosen Lagrangian.

Derivation and applications

How Hamiltonian flow preserves phase-space area

An energy contour shows where a single trajectory may lie. To learn what the dynamics does to nearby initial conditions, follow a small patch of the canonical \((q,p)\) plane. Consider one degree of freedom and a twice continuously differentiable Hamiltonian. Let the system advance by a short time \(\delta t\). To first order the coordinate map is a displacement along the Hamiltonian velocity field:

\[q'=q+H_p\,\delta t,\]
\[p'=p-H_q\,\delta t .\]

The area of a sufficiently small parallelogram changes by the determinant of this map's derivative. Differentiate with respect to both initial coordinates. The product of off-diagonal elements enters only at second order:

\[\det\frac{\partial(q',p')}{\partial(q,p)}=(1+H_{pq}\delta t)(1-H_{qp}\delta t)+H_{pp}H_{qq}\delta t^2=1+O(\delta t^2).\]

For the exact continuous map its area rate is the divergence of the phase velocity. Equality of the mixed second derivatives gives

\[\frac{1}{A}\frac{dA}{dt}=\partial_q\dot q+\partial_p\dot p=H_{pq}-H_{qp}=0\]

for an infinitesimal material area \(A\). Integrating adjacent patches gives the same conclusion for a finite region. The outline can stretch and rotate. Even explicit time dependence of \(H\) does not change this volume result. A friction force imposed independently of the Hamiltonian can change it. The second-order remainder above belongs to a short expansion; repeatedly taking finite steps with an approximate rule can accumulate a spurious change.

Worked example

Track a cloud in a harmonic trap

Prepare a small rectangular set of initial states, \(2.00\) mm wide in position and \(0.0100\) kg m/s wide in momentum. Its area is \(2.00\times10^{-5}\) J s. In a trap of mass \(0.500\) kg and frequency \(\omega=4.00\) rad/s, the exact quarter-period map is \(q'=p/(m\omega)\), \(p'=-m\omega q\). The new widths are \(5.00\) mm and \(0.00400\) kg m/s. Their product retains the original area even though neither width is conserved.

Second application

Diagnose a numerical phase portrait

An explicit Euler step in the same trap uses \(q'=q+p\delta t/m\) and \(p'=p-m\omega^2q\delta t\). Its determinant is exactly \(1+\omega^2\delta t^2\). At \(\delta t=0.0500\) s it expands area by \(1.04\) per step, giving \(1.04^{50}=7.11\) times the initial area after 50 steps. A graph of the growing cloud could look like physical heating. The determinant exposes a discretization error without requiring an exact trajectory for each point.

Position needs a conjugate momentum

The position of a pendulum at one instant does not say which way it will swing. A state needs both a configuration coordinate and a momentum. In Lagrangian mechanics define the canonical momentum by \(p=\partial\mathcal L/\partial\dot q\). It can have different physical dimensions depending on the coordinate. For a particle on a line it has units of linear momentum. For a pendulum angle it has units of angular momentum. The pair \((q,p)\) forms a two-dimensional phase space for one degree of freedom.

Provided the relation between \(p\) and \(\dot q\) can be inverted, define \(H(q,p,t)=p\dot q-\mathcal L(q,\dot q,t)\) with the velocity expressed in terms of \(q,p\). Take a differential. The terms proportional to \(d\dot q\) cancel by the definition of \(p\). Using Lagrange’s equation \(\partial_q\mathcal L=\dot p\) gives

\[dH=\dot q\,dp-\dot p\,dq-\partial_t\mathcal L\,dt.\]

Matching the coefficients produces Hamilton’s equations

\[\dot q=\partial_p H,\]
\[\dot p=-\partial_q H.\]

For a system with ordinary quadratic kinetic energy, a velocity-independent potential and fixed constraints, \(H=K+U\). Its change along a solution is \(dH/dt=\partial_tH\) because the two terms containing the derivatives with respect to \(q,p\) cancel. A time-independent Hamiltonian confines the trajectory to a level set.

Use Poisson brackets to test conservation

Hamilton's two equations specify how \(q_i\) and \(p_i\) change. To follow an observable \(A(q,p,t)\), apply the chain rule and substitute \(\dot q_i=\partial H/\partial p_i\) and \(\dot p_i=-\partial H/\partial q_i\). This defines the Poisson bracket,

\[\frac{dA}{dt}=\frac{\partial A}{\partial t}+\{A,H\},\]
\[\{A,B\}=\sum_i\left(\frac{\partial A}{\partial q_i}\frac{\partial B}{\partial p_i}-\frac{\partial A}{\partial p_i}\frac{\partial B}{\partial q_i}\right).\]

Since \(\{q_i,p_j\}=\delta_{ij}\), a candidate change of phase-space coordinates must preserve these brackets to be canonical. A vanishing bracket with \(H\) certifies conservation for an observable having no explicit time dependence. It is a local calculation and does not require solving an orbit.

For a particle in a two-dimensional trap set \(H=(p_x^2+p_y^2)/(2m)+(k_xx^2+k_yy^2)/2\) and \(L_z=xp_y-yp_x\). The momentum derivatives in the bracket cancel the two kinetic contributions. The remaining coordinate terms give \(\dot L_z=(k_x-k_y)xy\), exactly the torque \(xF_y-yF_x\). With \(k_x=4.00\) N/m, \(k_y=6.00\) N/m and instantaneous position \((0.030,0.040)\) m, angular momentum decreases at \(2.40\times10^{-3}\) N m. At a point on either axis the instantaneous derivative vanishes even for the anisotropic trap. For a conservation law holding on every trajectory the expression must vanish throughout phase space, as it does when \(k_x=k_y\).

Draw energy contours before integrating

A rigid pendulum of mass \(m\) and length \(\ell\) has \(p=m\ell^2\dot q\) and \(H=p^2/(2m\ell^2)+mg\ell(1-\cos q)\). At the bottom \(q=0,p=0\) is a stable equilibrium. Low-energy contours are ellipses to leading order because the cosine potential is quadratic there. Each closed contour returns to its original phase point after one full oscillation. Points of greatest \(|p|\) are fastest and occur at the bottom.

The top \(q=\pi,p=0\) has energy \(2mg\ell\). Its contour separates swinging trajectories from trajectories that keep rotating in one direction. This separating curve is the separatrix. Since angles differing by \(2\pi\) label the same physical position, the full portrait lives on a cylinder. A rotation winds around that cylinder. As a trajectory approaches the separatrix, it spends a long time near the unstable top, so the small-angle period no longer estimates its return time accurately.

Compare thresholds and phase velocities

For \(m=0.50\) kg, \(\ell=0.80\) m and \(g=9.81\) m/s², the potential barrier is 7.85 J. A start from the bottom at 5.0 rad/s supplies kinetic energy \(0.5(0.50)(0.80)^2(5.0)^2=4.00\) J. At the turning point \(\cos q=1-4.00/(0.50\times9.81\times0.80)=-0.0194\). The maximum angle is about 91.1°. Energy classifies this as a swing even though a small-angle expansion would be inaccurate at the turning point.

Continuous rotation requires bottom speed above \(\sqrt{4g/\ell}=7.00\) rad/s for this ideal pendulum. At precisely that value the system approaches the upper equilibrium asymptotically. This statement assumes a rigid link that can sustain compression. A pendulum made from a flexible string may lose tension before reaching the top, in which case the assumed circular constraint fails.

Check a numerical phase portrait

For any smooth one-degree Hamiltonian the divergence of the phase velocity is

\[\partial_q\dot q+\partial_p\dot p=H_{pq}-H_{qp}=0.\]

A small patch of initial states can shear and stretch while preserving its phase-space area. Viscous damping would instead contract the patch. Numerical time integration can also produce apparent contraction or growth, so refining the step is essential before interpreting a spiral as evidence of friction.

For the harmonic oscillator, ordinary forward Euler gives

\[q'=q+\Delta t\,p/m,\]
\[p'=p-\Delta t\,kq.\]

The determinant of this linear update is \(1+\Delta t^2 k/m\), larger than one at every step. The spurious outward spiral follows from the numerical map alone. Updating momentum first and then using the new momentum for the position gives determinant one. That area property preserves the geometric structure, though it does not by itself guarantee accurate phase for a coarse step. Keep both geometry and timing in view when judging numerical trajectories.

The area inside a closed harmonic-oscillator contour provides another numerical test. Its semiaxes are \(\sqrt{2E/k}\) in position and \(\sqrt{2mE}\) in momentum. Their product times \(\pi\) gives \(2\pi E/\omega\). Increasing oscillator energy therefore expands the area in direct proportion. A phase portrait plotted using velocity replaces every momentum value by its value divided by mass, changing numerical areas and their units. Specify the vertical coordinate before comparing this diagnostic between models with different masses.

A slowly changing spring transports a phase-space area

Suppose an oscillator's spring stiffness can be varied externally during the motion. Its Hamiltonian \(H=p^2/(2m)+m\omega(t)^2q^2/2\) now depends explicitly on time, so ordinary energy conservation does not apply. At an instantaneous point on a trajectory Hamilton's equations make the terms involving \(\dot p\) and \(\dot q\) cancel in \(dH/dt\). The remaining change is

\[dE/dt=\partial H/\partial t=m\omega\dot\omega q^2.\]

Energy is supplied when the imposed parameter raises the potential at the current displacement.

Take \(|\dot\omega|\ll\omega^2\) so that many cycles occur while the frequency changes by an appreciable fraction. Over one cycle \(\omega\) and \(E\) may then be treated as nearly fixed. Because average potential and kinetic energies of a harmonic oscillator are equal,

\[\langle q^2\rangle=E/(m\omega^2).\]

Inserting this mean into the work rate gives the averaged relation

\[\frac{dE}{dt}\simeq\frac{E}{\omega}\dot\omega,\qquad \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac E\omega\right)\simeq0 .\]

The quantity \(J=E/\omega\) is the area enclosed by the instantaneous constant-energy ellipse divided by \(2\pi\). Calling it an adiabatic invariant states both the approximation and the observable. A slowly changing system migrates between contours of different energies in a way that preserves this action to leading order. This mechanical meaning of adiabatic concerns a timescale ratio. The derivation does not assume heat exchange, dissipation or a thermodynamic temperature.

Compare a slow compression with a sudden change

A \(0.500\) kg oscillator has initial angular frequency \(20.0\) rad/s and amplitude \(0.0400\) m. Its energy is \(m\omega_i^2 A_i^2/2=0.160\) J and initial action is \(0.00800\) J s. Raise the frequency smoothly to \(40.0\) rad/s over many periods. Action conservation predicts \(E_f=0.320\) J and final amplitude \(\sqrt{2E_f/(m\omega_f^2)}=0.0283\) m. The external control supplies work even as the maximum displacement decreases.

For comparison, change the stiffness instantaneously at a turning point, retaining the same initial and final frequencies. Position and momentum are continuous, but the potential energy at that position quadruples immediately. The new energy is \(0.640\) J and the amplitude remains \(0.0400\) m. Make the same switch at the zero crossing instead and the energy initially remains \(0.160\) J because the potential vanishes there. These two outcomes distinguish a rapid change tied to an oscillator phase from the nearly phase-independent slow ramp. Recording both position and velocity after the ramp allows energy, action and phase to be assessed separately.

A time-dependent Hamiltonian still carries each small phase-space area element into one of equal area, even during a sudden change of curvature. This exact conservation for an ensemble is distinct from slow conservation of the contour area assigned to a single orbit. A group of uniformly distributed initial phases illustrates the difference: immediately after a sudden spring switch all points occupy the same locations and enclose their original region, but their new energies depend on phase. The switch maps the original single-energy contour onto points that subsequently occupy many different energy contours of the new Hamiltonian.

Nonlinear stiffness and amplitude-dependent period

Classical mechanics

Derive the leading frequency shift of a symmetric nonlinear oscillator and test whether a measured amplitude shift is consistent with a cubic spring.

From first principles

Derive the first amplitude correction to a spring’s frequency

A symmetric elastic restoring force has no quadratic displacement term; the first correction to a linear spring can therefore be cubic. Suppose a conservative oscillator satisfies \(m\ddot x+kx+\alpha x^3=0\) with \(k>0\) and \(|\alpha A^2/k|\ll1\) at measured peak amplitude \(A\). Leading motion is approximately \(A\cos\Omega t\). Cube this motion to find which frequencies enter the perturbing force:

\[x^3\simeq A^3[\tfrac34\cos\Omega t+\tfrac14\cos3\Omega t] .\]

To maintain a bounded periodic solution, its fundamental component must balance at first order. Otherwise the remaining fundamental force would resonantly grow the correction. Combine the inertia and restoring-force coefficients at \(\cos\Omega t\):

\[-m\Omega^2A+kA+\tfrac34\alpha A^3\simeq0,\qquad \Omega\simeq\sqrt{k/m}\left(1+\frac{3\alpha A^2}{8k}\right).\]

The third-harmonic component also distorts the waveform, but reinserting that order-\(\alpha\) distortion into the cubic force affects the fundamental only at the next perturbative order. Positive \(\alpha\) raises frequency with amplitude; negative \(\alpha\) lowers it within the local range where the stable weakly nonlinear model applies.

Derivation and applications

Find the third harmonic generated by a weak cubic spring

A change in oscillation period is one signature of a nonlinear restoring force. The same nonlinearity also changes the shape of the recorded motion. Let an undamped mass obey \(m\ddot x+kx+\alpha x^3=0\), with \(k>0\). Release it from rest at the measured peak displacement \(A\). Define \(\omega_0=\sqrt{k/m}\), the small signed parameter \(\epsilon=\alpha A^2/k\), and a stretched time \(\tau=\Omega t\). Write \(x/A=\cos\tau+\epsilon u(\tau)\) with \(u(0)=u'(0)=0\) so that \(A\) remains the physical turning point.

Allow the frequency to shift as \(\Omega=\omega_0(1+\epsilon\nu)\). Substitute the expansion into the equation and retain terms only to first order in \(\epsilon\). Primes below denote differentiation with respect to \(\tau\):

\[u''+u=2\nu\cos\tau-\cos^3\tau=(2\nu-3/4)\cos\tau-\tfrac14\cos3\tau .\]

A term driven at the unit frequency would grow proportionally to \(\tau\sin\tau\), contradicting a bounded, periodic correction. Choose \(\nu=3/8\) to remove it. The remaining forcing is at three times the fundamental. A trial \(C\cos3\tau\) has \(u''+u=-8C\cos3\tau\). Add the required homogeneous term to satisfy the turning-point conditions:

\[u(\tau)=\frac{\cos3\tau-\cos\tau}{32}.\]

The predicted displacement therefore contains two lines with linked amplitudes and a shifted fundamental:

\[x(t)\simeq A\!\left[(1-\epsilon/32)\cos\Omega t+(\epsilon/32)\cos3\Omega t\right],\qquad \Omega/\omega_0\simeq1+3\epsilon/8 .\]

The coefficient sign gives the third harmonic relative to the phase of a release at positive displacement. Squaring an amplitude to obtain a power spectrum erases that sign. The approximation assumes \(|\epsilon|\ll1\) and excludes appreciable dissipation during the observation.

Worked example

Estimate a micrometre-scale distortion

Take \(A=12.0\) mm, \(k=20.0\) N/m and \(\alpha=1.00\times10^4\) N/m³. Then \(\epsilon=0.0720\), so the third-harmonic displacement amplitude is

\[12.0(0.0720)/32=0.0270\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The fundamental frequency should increase by \(2.70\%\). With \(\omega_0=10.0\) rad/s, the third-harmonic line lies close to \(30.8\) rad/s.

Second application

Test whether a measured line comes from the spring

At \(A=6.00\) mm on the same apparatus, \(\epsilon\) falls by four and the third-harmonic amplitude falls by eight to \(3.38\) micrometres. The fractional frequency shift becomes \(0.675\%\). A third-harmonic signal that instead halves with amplitude could arise from an already distorted input or readout. The two amplitude scalings plus an independent frequency measurement test the cubic model more strongly than observing a line at three times the frequency.

Start from a reversible force law

A spring that looks linear under small deflection can stiffen as it is displaced further. For an oscillator symmetric under \(x\mapsto-x\), the restoring force expanded about equilibrium has no even powers. Keep the leading correction and write \(m\ddot x+kx+\alpha x^3=0\), with \(k>0\) and \(\alpha\) measured in N/m³. Assume the displacement is small enough that neglected fifth-order terms remain unimportant. Positive \(\alpha\) gives a hardening spring. A negative cubic coefficient describes local softening and may need higher-order terms to represent large displacements safely within the model.

The potential is \(kx^2/2+\alpha x^4/4\). Without damping the total energy is conserved and a release at amplitude \(A\) fixes that energy. Unlike a harmonic oscillator, the quadratic curvature at the origin no longer fixes the period for every possible energy. We want a quantitative connection between the amplitude of the motion and its zero-crossing interval.

Obtain the first frequency correction

Try \(x\simeq A\cos\Omega t\) and substitute it into the cubic equation. The identity \(\cos^3 u=(3\cos u+\cos3u)/4\) shows that the nonlinearity introduces a component at the original frequency and a third harmonic. Balance the fundamental coefficient to get \(-m\Omega^2+k+3\alpha A^2/4\simeq0\). Expanding the square root gives

\[\Omega\simeq\omega_0\left(1+\frac{3\alpha A^2}{8k}\right),\]
\[\omega_0=\sqrt{k/m}.\]

The control parameter is \(|\alpha|A^2/k\), the ratio of cubic to linear force at maximum displacement. The small additional third harmonic changes the waveform at first order but only feeds back into this leading frequency result at higher order. Fitting a perfect sinusoid to a high-precision trace may therefore show a residual even when the measured average period follows this formula.

Check the result using the conserved energy

The quarter-period is the time to move from equilibrium to \(A\). Substitution of the energy at the turning point gives an integral over \(dx/\sqrt{2[E-U(x)]/m}\). Let \(x=A\sin u\) to remove the square-root endpoint. The remaining integrand is proportional to \(\{1+[\alpha A^2/(2k)](1+\sin^2u)\}^{-1/2}\). Expanding and averaging over the interval from zero to \(\pi/2\) uses mean \(\sin^2 u=1/2\), yielding \(T\simeq(2\pi/\omega_0)[1-3\alpha A^2/(8k)]\). This agrees with the reciprocal of the harmonic-balance result.

The integral approach generalises to experimentally tabulated conservative forces by integrating them first to obtain a potential. It would need modification for strong damping, where energy varies significantly between the two turning points of one cycle.

Infer stiffness from period data

For \(m=0.50\) kg, \(k=200\) N/m and \(\alpha=4.0\times10^4\) N/m³, \(\omega_0=20.0\) rad/s. At \(A=0.020\) m the force ratio is 0.080, giving a leading frequency increase of 3.0% to 20.6 rad/s. At half this amplitude the predicted increase is 0.75%. Measure the period over several cycles at each amplitude, keeping the amplitude change due to loss small. A plot of frequency against the square of amplitude should be approximately straight near the origin. A nonzero intercept shift under a change of temperature could indicate a change in ordinary linear stiffness instead of a new nonlinear coefficient.

Compute the entire amplitude dependence of a pendulum

A cubic expansion is useful near equilibrium. For a pendulum it is possible to carry the energy calculation beyond every finite Taylor approximation. Take a point mass with fixed length \(\ell\), no friction and a release from rest at angle \(0<\theta_0<\pi\). Its conserved energy gives

\[\dot\theta^2=(2g/\ell)(\cos\theta-\cos\theta_0).\]

Use \(\cos\theta=1-2\sin^2(\theta/2)\). Defining \(k=\sin(\theta_0/2)\) reduces the speed magnitude to \(2\sqrt{g/\ell}\sqrt{k^2-\sin^2(\theta/2)}\).

One quarter period is the time to travel between zero angle and the turning point. The reciprocal speed appears to diverge at the latter, but it has an integrable square-root singularity for every ordinary turning angle. To evaluate it stably, set \(\sin(\theta/2)=k\sin u\). The factor \(\cos u\) from differentiating cancels the same factor inside the square root. Four equivalent quarters give

\[T=4\sqrt{\ell/g}\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{du}{\sqrt{1-k^2\sin^2u}}=4\sqrt{\ell/g}\,K(k).\]

Here \(K\) denotes the complete elliptic integral with modulus \(k\). Expanding the integrand at small \(k\) and integrating the first correction gives

\[T/T_0=1+k^2/4+\cdots=1+\theta_0^2/16+\cdots,\]

where \(T_0=2\pi\sqrt{\ell/g}\). The positive correction is the familiar softening: increasing amplitude lengthens the period because the restoring torque grows more slowly than its linear extrapolation.

Decide when a small-angle clock loses accuracy

A pendulum of length \(0.800\) m has \(T_0=1.794\) s at \(g=9.81\) m/s². At a release of \(60.0^\circ\),

\[k=\sin30^\circ=0.500.\]

Evaluating the integral gives \(K(0.500)=1.68575\) and \(T/T_0=2K/\pi=1.0732\), hence \(T=1.926\) s. The weak-angle approximation gives

\[1+(\pi/3)^2/16=1.0685.\]

It misses the exact period by roughly \(0.008\) s per cycle; a many-cycle timing record resolves the accumulated discrepancy.

As \(\theta_0\) tends to \(\pi\), the integrand near \(u=\pi/2\) approaches the reciprocal of a vanishing cosine. The integral diverges logarithmically, expressing the long time spent close to the unstable upper equilibrium. No finite polynomial in amplitude can reproduce that divergence. A numerical timing experiment should therefore locate regular zero crossings rather than demand a turning-point velocity of exactly zero in sampled data. Comparing the crossing intervals with the regularized integral distinguishes numerical resolution error from the genuine increase in physical period.

A way to evaluate the elliptic integral without a special-function library is to form the arithmetic–geometric mean. Begin with \(a_0=1\) and \(b_0=\sqrt{1-k^2}\), then replace the pair by their arithmetic and geometric means. Both converge rapidly to a common \(M\), and \(K(k)=\pi/(2M)\). For the 60° release the first pair is \(1\) and \(0.86603\), the next pair is approximately \(0.93301\) and \(0.93060\), and the subsequent pair agrees near \(0.93181\). Taking the reciprocal of the common value directly gives the period ratio. This procedure supplies an accurate independent benchmark even when the small-amplitude expansion ceases to converge quickly.

Numerical quadrature and the arithmetic–geometric mean should agree at a fixed amplitude. If a period extracted from actual trajectories differs while these two evaluations agree, examine the dynamical time step, how release angle is measured, and whether the observed oscillations have suffered appreciable damping.

Liouville flow and what an ensemble can remember

Classical mechanics

A cloud of identical noninteracting beads is released with slightly different initial positions and momenta. Its position distribution widens. Does the system occupy more phase space, or has its uncertainty merely changed shape?

Follow a small phase-space element

For one canonical degree of freedom the velocity of a point \((q,p)\) is \((\partial_pH,-\partial_qH)\). A short-time map takes \(\boldsymbol z\) to \(\boldsymbol z+\boldsymbol v(\boldsymbol z,t)\,dt\). Its Jacobian matrix is \(I+(\partial\boldsymbol v/\partial\boldsymbol z)dt\); expanding the determinant gives \(1+(\nabla_z\cdot\boldsymbol v)dt+O(dt^2)\). Hamilton's equations make the divergence zero because the two mixed second derivatives of a smooth Hamiltonian cancel. Each additional canonical pair contributes a similar cancellation. A time-dependent Hamiltonian may change energy but still preserve the instantaneous phase-space volume along the flow.

Conservation of the number of ensemble members in a moving phase-space box also gives the continuity equation \(\partial_t f+\nabla_z\cdot(f\boldsymbol v)=0\). The vanishing divergence leaves \(df/dt=0\) on each trajectory. A stationary mechanical system can admit \(f=F(H)\) as a stationary distribution because trajectories stay on energy surfaces. Such stationarity alone does not imply that one trajectory explores an entire energy surface. For example, further integrals of motion restrict regular multidimensional trajectories.

Propagate a cloud released into free flight

For mass \(m\), the exact map is \(p=p_0,\ q=q_0+tp_0/m\). Its determinant is one regardless of time. An initially uncorrelated cloud with variances \(s_q^2,s_p^2\) has evolved covariance \(C_{qp}=ts_p^2/m\). The new position variance is \(s_q^2+(ts_p/m)^2\), while the momentum variance stays fixed. Compute the determinant of this covariance matrix:

\[\sigma_q^2\sigma_p^2-C_{qp}^2=s_q^2s_p^2 .\]

For a Gaussian the ellipse at a fixed multiple of the covariance radius thus retains its area while it shears. With \(m=1\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(s_q=1\,\mathrm m\), \(s_p=0.5\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}\), and \(t=4\,\mathrm s\), the observed position standard deviation is \(\sqrt5=2.236\,\mathrm m\). The momentum standard deviation remains 0.5 and the covariance becomes \(1.0\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}\). Their uncorrected width product is 1.118 in action units. The square root of the covariance determinant is still 0.5. Multiplication of measured marginal widths alone falsely suggests an area increase.

Compare reversible shear and real drag

Now apply a linear drag \(\dot p=-\gamma p\) to each bead with constant \(\gamma>0\). Its phase-space divergence is \(-\gamma\), hence the map determinant after time \(t\) is \(e^{-\gamma t}\). Direct integration gives

\[p=e^{-\gamma t}p_0,\ q=q_0+(1-e^{-\gamma t})p_0/(m\gamma).\]

The cloud now genuinely loses area in the reduced bead description. A larger Hamiltonian model of bead plus environment can remain volume preserving. Choose a drag rate in the graph and compare the original, freely sheared and damped ellipses for the same observation time. Check there is a smooth zero-drag limit rather than substituting zero into a quotient.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on distinguishing a shear from dissipation

A harmonic oscillator maps its scaled quadratures by a rotation. Show why an initially anisotropic cloud can periodically sharpen in position without phase-space cooling.

Work through the solution

Write its covariance as \(R\Sigma_0R^T\). Since \(\det R=1\), its determinant is unchanged. A rotation of one quarter turn exchanges the original widths, so position can sharpen when the original momentum width in scaled units was smaller. Energy extraction is unnecessary.

At \(t=3\,\mathrm s\) the measured ellipse area is 55 percent of the initial area in the linear-drag model. Infer \(\gamma\). Which independent observable checks the inference?

Work through the solution
\[\gamma=-\ln(0.55)/3=0.199\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}.\]

The ratio of final to initial momentum standard deviations should also be 0.55 for drag without random forcing. Added environmental fluctuations would modify that prediction.

Further reading · Cambridge classical dynamics, Hamiltonian flow and Liouville's theorem.

Parametric resonance and Floquet growth

Classical mechanics

A pendulum can gain energy when its length varies periodically even if no additive force acts on it. A modulation near twice a natural frequency amplifies one phase of the oscillation and attenuates another. The stability test concerns the growth of a perturbation over a complete modulation period.

Linearise the timing mechanism

Consider \(\ddot x+2\gamma\dot x+\omega_0^2[1+h\cos(\Omega t)]x=0\). Here \(h\) is the fractional modulation of the stiffness and \(\gamma\) is the amplitude damping rate. If \(x=\dot x=0\) exactly, the solution stays zero. Small fluctuations or an initial condition provide the seed. Direct differentiation of \(E=[\dot x^2+\omega_0^2(1+h\cos\Omega t)x^2]/2\) yields

\[\dot E=-2\gamma\dot x^2-h\omega_0^2\Omega x^2\sin(\Omega t)/2.\]

Energy transfer depends on the phase of \(x^2\), which naturally contains a component at twice the oscillation frequency.

Near \(\nu=\Omega/2\simeq\omega_0\), write \(x=A(t)e^{i\nu t}+A^*(t)e^{-i\nu t}\) with slowly varying \(A\). Multiplication by \(\cos(2\nu t)\) mixes the negative-frequency term back into the positive-frequency term with coefficient \(h\omega_0^2/2\). There is also a component at \(3\nu\), rapidly oscillating in this first-resonance approximation. Retain terms proportional to \(e^{i\nu t}\), neglect \(\ddot A\) and products of small damping with \(\dot A\). This gives

\[2i\nu\dot A+(\omega_0^2-\nu^2+2i\gamma\nu)A+(h\omega_0^2/2)A^*=0.\]

Find the first instability tongue

Set \(\Delta=\nu-\omega_0\) and keep leading terms in the small ratios \(h,\gamma/\omega_0,|\Delta|/\omega_0\). The slow equations are \(\dot A=(-\gamma-i\Delta)A+igA^*\) and their conjugate, where \(g=h\omega_0/4\). The two-by-two characteristic equation is

\[(s+\gamma)^2=g^2-\Delta^2.\]

The faster exponent is

\[\operatorname{Re}s_+=-\gamma+\operatorname{Re}\sqrt{(h\omega_0/4)^2-\Delta^2}.\]

The oscillation grows when \(g^2>\gamma^2+\Delta^2\). Exactly on resonance this gives \(h>4\gamma/\omega_0\). With \(\omega_0=10\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\), \(\gamma=0.10\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\) and \(h=0.08\), \(g=0.20\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\), giving an on-resonance doubling time \(\ln2/0.10=6.93\,\mathrm s\). The approximate growing interval in drive angular frequency is \(20\pm0.346\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). A large-amplitude real oscillator can saturate as its natural frequency changes with amplitude, a mechanism absent from this linear equation.

Compute the period map without averaging

Integrate the original linear equation over one drive period from two independent initial states \((1,0)\) and \((0,1)\). Their final columns form a matrix \(M\), which maps every initial perturbation over the same period \(T\). After \(j\) periods the map is \(M^j\); the maximum asymptotic amplitude exponent is \(\ln\max|\lambda_i(M)|/T\). Its determinant also has an exact check \(e^{-2\gamma T}\) from the divergence in the \((x,\dot x)\) plane. The calculation below integrates both columns by fourth-order Runge–Kutta. Compare the numerical Floquet exponent with the averaged prediction as the modulation depth increases. Both curves describe growth rates, including negative rates for decay.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on phase-sensitive amplification

At exact resonance and zero loss, substitute \(A=X+iY\) into \(\dot A=igA^*\). Identify the two special quadrature directions.

Work through the solution

Equating real and imaginary parts yields

\[\dot X=gY,\ \dot Y=gX.\]

Along \(Y=X\) both grow at \(g\), whereas along \(Y=-X\) they decay at \(g\). These directions reflect how timing the stiffness change can add or remove work on each cycle.

On resonance an oscillator with \(\omega_0=40\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\) first grows near \(h=0.03\). Estimate its small-signal damping and its free amplitude e-folding time.

Work through the solution

From the weak-modulation threshold \(\gamma=h\omega_0/4=0.30\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). Without modulation the underdamped free amplitude falls as \(e^{-\gamma t}\), giving \(3.33\,\mathrm s\). Threshold uncertainty or nonlinear frequency shifts propagate into this inference.

Further reading · Verhulst, Utrecht University, perturbation analysis of parametric resonance.

A magnetic orbit that spirals outward in a perfect vacuum

Numerical field laboratory

Track a charged particle in a prescribed uniform field, derive a rotation update and separate numerical energy growth from a real magnetic force.

Use the work law before solving the trajectory

A nonrelativistic charged particle of mass \(m\) and charge \(q\) moves perpendicular to a uniform, time-independent magnetic field \(B\hat{\mathbf z}\). There is no electric field and the dilute test particles do not affect the applied field. The Lorentz force gives \(\dot v_x=\omega v_y,\ \dot v_y=-\omega v_x\) for \(\omega=qB/m\). Dotting with \(\mathbf v\) gives zero, so the speed should stay constant. This property holds for either sign of the charge; the direction of rotation changes. It is a better initial test of a trajectory solver than how circular a short segment looks on screen.

Define \(J(v_x,v_y)=(v_y,-v_x)\), so \(J^T=-J\) and \(J^2=-I\). The exact velocity over a time step \(h\) is \([\cos(\omega h)I+\sin(\omega h)J]\mathbf v\). Integration in time gives a circular orbit of radius \(v_\perp/|\omega|\) once the guiding centre is chosen. A magnetic field alone bends a trajectory without changing its kinetic energy; a slowly growing orbit radius at fixed \(B\) and fixed initial speed demands an explanation.

Why forward Euler adds energy at every step

Euler gives \(\mathbf v_+=(I+dJ)\mathbf v\) with \(d=\omega h\). Squaring the norm eliminates the cross term because \(v^TJv=0\), leaving \(\|\mathbf v_+\|^2=(1+d^2)\|\mathbf v\|^2\). No nonzero step has exact unit gain. A geometric picture makes the bias tangible: a tangent step leaves the initial velocity circle on its outside, whatever the point of departure.

After \(N\) steps the speed factor is \((1+d^2)^{N/2}\). With 24 steps per physical orbit, \(d=2\pi/24\simeq0.2618\), so a full orbit multiplies speed by roughly 2.2. At shorter times or smaller steps this can resemble weak physical acceleration. Reducing \(h\) at fixed physical duration reduces the growth. Testing one step at fixed count, rather than comparing over a fixed physical interval, underestimates the cumulative error.

Solve a centred magnetic step exactly

Use the time-centred velocity in the Lorentz force:

\[\mathbf v_+-\mathbf v=d J(\mathbf v_++\mathbf v)/2.\]

Premultiply by the transpose of \(\mathbf v_++\mathbf v\). The left side is the difference of squared speeds and the right side vanishes, so the update preserves speed for every \(d\) in exact arithmetic. Rearranging yields a two-dimensional linear solve; using \(J^2=-I\) turns it into a rotation,

\[\mathbf v_+=\frac{1-t^2}{1+t^2}\mathbf v+\frac{2t}{1+t^2}J\mathbf v,\]
\[t=d/2.\]

These coefficients are \(\cos\alpha\) and \(\sin\alpha\) for \(\alpha=2\arctan(d/2)\). This is the magnetic rotation used in the nonrelativistic Boris integrator. For simultaneous electric fields, the usual split step adds two half electric impulses around the magnetic rotation. The current experiment isolates the magnetic part; it makes no claim about a nonuniform electric field or the accuracy of interpolation from a field mesh to particles.

Open the live experiment

Speed conservation does not fix arrival time

At small \(d\),

\[\alpha=d-d^3/12+O(d^5).\]

A speed-preserving orbit lags in gyrophase, even when its energy plot stays perfectly flat. After four true orbits at 24 steps per orbit the predicted lag is about 8.14 degrees. The browser advances the Euler and rotation velocities and reports both norm and lag. The y-axis rescales to fit both curves, so a sharply growing Euler error can flatten the appearance of the well-behaved curve. Inspect numerical values as well as the picture.

Suppose an optical pickup observes the time at which a dilute ion tracer first crosses a known line of sight after several orbits. A phase error can be an important measurement error even when the speed is exact. To use the trajectory as a reference, refine the step until the crossing time changes by less than the experimental timing resolution. If the required integration spans many revolutions, a uniform field permits exact substeps. In realistic fields one can retain a structure-preserving rotation while resolving spatial variation independently.

A separate diagnostic is time reversal. Starting from the final velocity and applying the rotation with \(-d\) exactly inverts a centred step, up to rounding. Forward Euler with \(-d\) after \(+d\) gives

\[(I-dJ)(I+dJ)=(1+d^2)I.\]

A forward/backward test detects the lost reversibility without knowing a single experimental constant.

Tutorial

Given an orbit with 48 steps per period, estimate the gyrophase error after ten periods using the leading asymptotic term.

Work through the solution

There are 480 steps with \(d=2\pi/48\). The accumulated lag is about \(480d^3/12\simeq0.0897\) rad, or 5.14 degrees. Doubling the number of steps per period reduces the leading accumulated error fourfold.

A computation preserves speed but reverses the sign of its orbit relative to observation. Would the magnetic work test detect the error?

Work through the solution

Both field signs give zero work. Check a signed initial acceleration component \(a_y=-qBv_x/m\) for a positive initial \(v_x\), or observe the oriented trajectory. A scalar invariant cannot detect all vector-sign errors.

What changes in the relativistic case with appreciable kinetic energy?

Work through the solution

Evolve momentum \(\mathbf p=\gamma m\mathbf v\) and infer velocity from momentum. In a uniform magnetic field without electric work \(\gamma\) stays constant, and the orbital frequency is reduced to \(|q|B/(\gamma m)\). An energy-preserving nonrelativistic integration at the wrong frequency would still give the wrong measured period.

Relativistic particle dynamics and the field tensor

Waves, radiation and relativity

A circular track measures momentum per charge. A clock following the particle registers proper time, and a detector records how much kinetic energy it has gained. We will put these observations in one dynamical equation, solve that equation in uniform fields and exhibit a numerical pusher that can get the energy right while its orbit phase slowly slips.

What the track, clock and calorimeter measure

Consider a particle of invariant mass \(m\), signed charge \(q\) and ordinary velocity \(\mathbf v\). Inertial observers agree on the proper time between neighbouring events on its timelike worldline,

\[d\tau=dt\sqrt{1-v^2/c^2}.\]

Adopt coordinates \(x^\mu=(ct,x,y,z)\) and metric \(g=\mathrm{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1)\). Dividing the displacement by proper time produces a vector of fixed Minkowski norm. Multiplying it by invariant mass gives four-momentum.

\[u^\mu=\gamma(c,\mathbf v),\]
\[p^\mu=mu^\mu=(\mathcal E/c,\mathbf p),\]
\[\mathcal E^2-c^2|\mathbf p|^2=m^2c^4 .\]

Energy includes the rest term. Momentum is \(\gamma m\mathbf v\). Differentiating the last equation on a trajectory gives

\[d\mathcal E/dt=\mathbf v\cdot d\mathbf p/dt.\]

This identity replaces the nonrelativistic step of dotting \(m\mathbf a\) with velocity. A magnetic field can rotate momentum with fixed magnitude and energy, whereas an electric field can change energy by doing work.

Suppose an electron begins at rest and gains 10.0 keV of kinetic energy across an electrostatic potential difference in vacuum. Its rest energy is 511 keV, giving \(\gamma=1.01957\) and \(v/c=\sqrt{1-1/\gamma^2}=0.1950\). The nonrelativistic estimate is 0.1978. In a modest energy measurement the discrepancy could lie below the experimental spread. For a precise orbit calculation the 1.96 percent change in relativistic cyclotron period is measurable independently of that discrepancy in speed.
